THE CYCLE IN BUSINESS.

(Qaeriee and correspondence should be addressed to the Editor, do "Spokes." Replies can- not be sent by post, and all queries will be answered in our columns.)

The bicycle in business has made enormous progress during the last few years, antl especially during the last twelve months, where horse flesh has been dear, feeding stuffs almost prohibitive, and labour extremely scarce. This is more noticeable in rural districts, perhaps, than it is in the towns, although in the latter cycles for business purposes are every day in- creasing enormously in number. The initial cost is low; the upkeep next to nothing. The saving of time which can be affected is very striking. A small order has to be executed in a hurry. It is not only small parcels that can be carried. Quite heavy loads can be conveyed. whether a cycle or tricycle is used. There is a further advantage in the cycle carrier at the present moment when labour everywhere is so short. Girls who could not be trusted with/ a horse and cart, Are quite able to manage a cycle satisfactorily. Thousands all over the country testify to this. Everywhere one meets with girls of all ages and sizes cycling merrily along, seemingly making a pleasure of their work. The cycle for business purposes has come stay.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0010.txt | included=true | included =====
SOME " BELT " -RULES.

A belt should never be so tight as to cause 1 jerking when the engine is gathering speed from a standstill. Mudguards should be so arranged that no water drips from them on to the belt. and no mud or water can be splashed on it. The pulley and belt drum should be wide enough to allow the belt to bed down well into the groove so that the flanges can ward off any oil or water that is flung about from the engine and road wheels. The belt should never ride .on the bottom of pulley or belt drum, how- ever. Holes for the fastener should never be driven with a gimlet or bradawl, but always with a belt punch. Holes that are not square will cause the fastener screws to pull through Never dress leather belts with castor oil, always with eollan oil. Always run the belt off th,. belt drum when the machine is standing idle. :Use an adjustable fastener; it will save 60 per cent. of your shortening operations. When shortening, shorten each end alternately, so that each fastener hole is renewed alternately.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0011.txt | included=true | included =====
SPARE PLUGS.

Every careful driver of a motor car earn.% one or more spare sparking plugs. This is all very well so far as it goes. But nearly everyone i 4 • very careless in looking after them. More often , than not they are thrown into the tool box or beneath the seat, mixed up with all the other impedimenta. A sparking plug is really a • delicate piece of work, and very quickly be- comes damaged, when if not useless its effi- ciency is greatly impaired. A very simple device will prevent damage. T use the metal box in which shaving soap is purchased. A small wad of felt or cotton wool, at either end. will hinder injury, and the plug can be relied upon to keep in good working condition. The cardboard box in which an upright gas mantle has been packed also answers the purpose. but the metal box is better. -In the garage at home one usually has a few extra sparking plugs, and these reauire looking after as well. For storing them I have a piece of soft wood a foot long

TARRED ROADS AND MIST.

I really believe that the mist encountered in so many places nowadays during and after the rain is caused by the tarred road surfayes. I should like to know whether any of my readers have observed the same thing. I have fre- quently noticed that in the same stretch of road; if one part be gravel or flint and another part tarred, the mist only rises over the latter section of the road. Ido not know enough about physics to be able to explain the reason for the mist, but I do know it has something to do with the vapour tensions of the water. This should be a suitable subject for discussion by the experts of the Road Board, in conjunc- tion with those of the National Physical Labora- tory. If is certainly a point that might be kept in view with reference to road-surfacing materials. Such patches of mist are extremely dangerous, and if they can be overcome so much the better,

IT'S A MANLY ACT.

Runcorn Man Speaks Out.

A plain straightforward statement. A grateful acknowledgment of help.

Telling a personal experience. Making it everywhere known. It's a manly act. It's manly because it helps others by removing every doubt. No better help could be wished than this assurance from Runcorn.

On March 10th, 1915, Mr. J. Clucas, of 3, Cart- wright-street, Halton-road, Runcorn, said :—" I have been a martyr in my time to sciatica. I was once so bad with it that I was unable to do any work for eleven months. Even after I did get about again I was continually having bad bouts. The pains used to run down the sciatica nerve from my right hip to my ankle, and I was properly crippled with it. "I tried embrocations and all kinds or treat- ment, but I can truly say that nothing did me any lasting good until I started taking Doan's Backache Kidney Pills. I was very bad when I started with these pills', but they soon gave me relief, and as I persevered with them the pain gradually left me. Whenever I get a touch of the old trouble now I take,a few of these pills, for they always give me immediate relief." (Signed) "J. Clucas." On February 23rd, 1916—nearly twelve months inter—Mr. Clucas said :—" I still have the same high opinion of Dean's Pills. They have been worth pounds to me, and I always recommend them."

Of all dealers, or 2/9 a box, from Foster- McClellan Co.. 8, Wells-street, Oxford-street, London, W. Don't ask for backache or kidney pills,—ask diatinctly for Doan's Backache Kidney Pills, the same as Mr. Clneas had.

BOVRIL.

In the High Court of Justice, Chancery Division, Mr. Justice Eve granted Bovril (Limited) an injunction against a limited com- pany restraining them from passing off as Bovril, or in response to order for Bovril, any other meat preparation. The plaintiff company complained that in response to orders for Bovril, Oxo had on numerous occasions been substituted by the defendant company without comment or ex- planation. This the defendant company denied. An injunction was granted as stated, and the defendant company was ordered to pay the costs of the action.

TE-1E FORESTERS.

North-Western Federation Meets at Warrington.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
TRADE UN lONS AS COMPETITORS.

Warrington was honoured on Saturday by the presence of about forty delegates representit% the North-Western Federation of the Ancient Order of Foresters, who discussed chiefly the future of the Order, the subject being Intro- duced by Bro. W. Hipkiss, P.D.C.R. (of War-

rington). Their meeting was held in the Council Chamber at the Town Hail, to which they were welcomed by the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock), who is himself an enthusiastic Forester.

„Jse that doctor was more popular than her might be. Juveniles as Reinforcements. Alert) was remitted from the last meeting the -estion of the amalgamation of the Federation .tid the Juvenile Federal Council. The Juimittees appointed to consider it met the ~her Saturday afternoon, and decided to recom- dend that the two organisations ;should be _liked together. Resolutions on the subject, iould be submitted to that meeting. It was aggested that four members be appointed from ',lie Federation and four from, the Federal Council—eight in all—to work together for the xtension, if possible, of the juvenile member- hip by visiting courts and districts and en- leavouring to work up some enthusiasm in the .;reat work of the juvenile. organisation. These risits were to take place in the Federation area the committee to report at the Federation meet- ings on the work done. He thought the juv.mile work was a grand work. It helped to feed .the Courts and to make good the large secessions Ind depletions in the Order. (Hear, hear.) They would find, on looking into the matter, there had been a great number of secessions, very great indeed, and it called for very serious consideration. They ought to look very closely indeed into the causes.” There was no telling what they might be, possibly something which might be remedied. They were not, however, the only society with a large leakage. At the Friendly Societies' Con- ference a loss was reported of 151,959 for 1914. There must be some great cause for this serious depletion. Tithes changed, it was said. If they had changed in regard • to the spirit per- meating friendly society work they ought- to take into Consideration the spirit prevailing to- day, and endeavour, if possible, to bring their great work up to 'date. (Hear, hear.) Some societies were giving advantage's which the whole of the friendly societies were not giving. They had these societies to contend with. If they wished to stop the leakage in their Order let•them see if they could not give equal, if not better, advantages than those given by othe.2 organisations. Friendly Societies Unite the Colonies. As friendly societies and as Foresters, they had accomplished a great deal, They had not been in existence for close upon two centuries for a mere nothing. They had worked hard, and done useful work in the -country. Thes had a large membership and a large accumula- tion of funds. But there had been something far nobler done. They had helped, and possiblf might have been the instructors, in unititet to- gether the Colonies and our nation to-day. (Hear, hear.) Our Colonies had the sante organisations as we had, the same frien ily societies. They had been initiated-es brothers in their ranks, and had they not helped to spread and to make the great human brother- hood in connection with the frien.ily society world? They had united with the Colonies to make a great Empire by the brotherhood of humanity. (Applause.) ~As proof, he had only to -point to the great help the Colonies had given in the war. Their sons had come across the sea to uphold the honour and liberty cf Old England. (Hear, hear.) They were fight- ing for us to-day, and many of them were their brothers in the friendly society movement. The friendly societies, he claimed, had • played . no mean part in the building up of our mighty Empire. They were bound together in the same brotherhood, to help each other in adversity. (Applause.) As to the future, they had a great outlook. After the war nations that had not friendly society organisations would want to have &mu, and would welcome, them with open arms. Let them play their part as men, doing with all their might whatever their hands found to do, so that the world should be nobler for their having lived in it. It them strive with un- selfish devotion to make the world a better world, and might they live in unity, benevo- .ence, and concord. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19160401_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
AN OUT-AND-OUT FORESTER.

The delegates having assembled Bro. George Maybury, P.D.C.R., of Warrington, took the. chair, and the Mayor sat on his right. The Chairman said the Mayor had attended ' to give them an official welcome. To many of them his Worship needed no introduction, from him, for they had met hinf many times at High Court and Federation meetings. The Mayor was an put-and-out Forester, and not only so, but a great believer and supporter of the friendly society movement generally. He did not think there was a single society in the town with which his Worship was not connected in some way or other. (Hear, hear.) He was sure they would be very pleased to he welcomed by a brother Forester. (Applause.).

===== 0002601_19160401_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
MAYOR ALWAYS BEEN A FORESTER.

The Mayor, who was very cordially received, said it ,was very pleasing to him to be able to welcome his brother Foresters present as dele- gates from various parts of Lancashire, Cheshire, Derbyshire, Cumberland, and North Wales. It was very gratifying to him to do 60 in his official- capacity as Mayor and as a brother Forester. (Hear, hear.) He reminded them that it was ten years ago since they were , last welcomed in that building; then by one of his predecessors, Alderman Bolton. He well remembered the dayobecaulie after the business 'meeting • they had a social gathering at the Lion Hotel, which gave a considerable fillip to the friendly society movement in the town. His connection with Forestry dated from the time it was possible for him to enter any society, that was when he became 16 years of age. (Hear, hear.) They had no Juvenile Court then, but he Was a member .of the Juvenile Burial Society from the time he was one or two years old, so that his connection with Forestry dated back to his earliest years. (Applause.) As the chairman had said, he was connected with any and every society whose objects were the benefit of the community at large. He ! could not give them a better welcome than a brotherly one, and he hoped their deliberations would be fruitful and beneficial to their society. and ho hoped they would take back with them Plea-sant recollections of their Visit to Warring- ton. (Apjilause.) Bro. Hollins, District Chief Ranger, also ex- tended a hearty welcome to the delegates. They had very pleasant recollections of their last visit ten years ago. Since then, there had been great changes which meant that they would have to give their undivided and earnest atten- tion to their affairs in case of trouble which threatened. He was sure that day's delibera- tions would assist their ancient Order in the terrible trial which was before them all. He hoped the delegates would leave this ancient town with pleasant recollections of this visit. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19160401_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
A DOUBLE PLEASURE.

Bro. 3. Whiteside, P.H.C.T.' treasurer, moved a vote of thanks to the Mayor for the cordial reception he had given the dele- gates. Wherever they had their. Federation meetings they were always .pleased to have the countenance and recognition of the chief civic official. But he felt that on this occasion there was a double pleasure from the fact that their welcome had come not only from Bro. Peacock as Mayor, but as a brother Forester, and a benefit member at that. (Hear, hear.) They knew that his Worship had the highest admira- tion of the work being done by the society, and that he was doing all he could to help and, along with all of them, doing his best to help thrift and promote brotherhood amongst them. (Applause.) Bro. Harris, Chester; seconded, and said they were honoured by being welcomed by a working Forester. WheiLhe told them that he was the District Treasurer they would understand some- thing of the work he was doing. He congratu- lated Bro. Peacock upon what he had done for Forestry and friendly societies generally. His re-election as Mayor proved that he was also doing good work for the town, and he hoped he would be in , the future as successful in his endeavours for the benefit of the human race as in the past. (Applause.) The motion was carried with acclamation. The Mayor thanked Councillor Whiteside and Bro. Harris for their kind remarks. He was reminded of three years ago, when he met Councillor Whiteside at the time he was Chief Magistrate of Stretford, and they attended various functions together. He was gratified. that he should have been chosen to propose this vote of thanks. Bro. Harris he had met on about eight occasions, and found that he was doing a very useful work for the Order. The Mayor concluded by saying he had always taken a pleasure in doing what ,he could to further friendly society work, naturally in particular, in connection with Forestry. (Applause.)

BRO. MAYBURY ON DOCTORS AND INSURANCE ACT.

The Chairman said it gave him very great pleasure to join with the Mayor and the District Chief Ranger in giving them a very hearty welcome to Warrington.

- This year, continued the Chairman, happened to be the 21st anniversary of the fprmation•of the Federation. It would be '2l years • ago in June since this North-Western Federation was brought into being. (Hear, hear.) It was pre- viously known as the Manchester and 'District Union. He saw some present who were at the great meeting at Chester when the change was brought about. The work of the Federation during the 21 years had not been in vain. They "Ad on many' occasions assisted districts to secure the High Courts, assisted members to obtain `High Court honours, and in other ways showed that good work had been done. .The Federation would have greater scope for useful- ness in the future, because they might be called upon, not as a Federation, but as an organised ot combined body to consider some important

proposals. There had been a great deal of discussion and correspondence of late with regard to medical. treatment under the National Insurance Act Letters had been published in various" papers as to certain medical men having a very largo proportion of National State Insurance members on their panel. Resolutions had been passed regarding providing medical practitioners for State insured members only: During the war there was a considerable. shortage of medical practitioners, as many were away • serving their country. But as to those left behind, he did not think they could, as a friendly society, .begrudge them the vast number of . meMber, they had on their panels. There were vat-ions systems. The visit system was in Vogue in some large central districts, where the funds of the society were pooled, and the doctor had to visit the Patient.- .There was a .great deal to be said in its favour. Then there Was the panel system, which he considered was one of the best, because it gave to each and every indi • vidual member the right to choose his own medical man tO attend him while sick, lie maintained that the doctors had endeavoured to discharge .what was a very responsible duty with conscientiousness and integrity. • (Hear, hear.) They must not blame the medical men if members kept upon their funds a long; time. The great object, practically speaking, was not to relieve sickness or, pain, but for the medical man to cure it, and he thought they ought as an Order to support the panel system, because, as he had said, it gave every member the right to choose his own medical man. If one doctor had more patients, than another it was simply

===== 0002601_19160401_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FUTURE OF THE ORDER?

What w\as described as a thoughtful and far- reaching paper was then read by Bro. Hipkiss. He said it was well for them, perhaps, especially at this particular and anxious period of their history, to examine their position—even at the risk of a tendency to pessimism—in the hope ; of revealing effective and lasting remedies for some, at least, of their shortcomings and failures. They, too often, perhaps, in their Society News, read only of the lighter social side of the Order, of those bright and happy functions, very desirable perhaps, and often even necessary, but at the same time rather cal- culated to deceive and mislead them as to the true state of things. They. did not as a rule read of, or even hear much of, the struggling branches, with their anxious officers, :often in splendid isolation, looking eagerly for some re- laxation of their heavy responsibilities, airi wondering "What of the future?"' They were justified in taking pride in the glorious history of their Order, for that noble monument to the thrift and self-governing abilities displayed by ' the workingmen of the last century, but their infatuation for a glorious past should not prevent the necessary appreciation , in the requirements of the future.. It had been stated by some of the champions of the independence of branch life that "the present-day enthusiasm is not sufficiently strong to steer all branches to success." That was undoubtedly true, for, generally, their branches had failed to appr2- ciate the need for, much less the adoption of, the necessary reforms which from time to time had been devised to obviate and retard the numerical .deeline, which they depineed, with the result that often they had found themselves on the verge of disaster before essential remedial measures could be applied. Districts had lacked the. same qualities, and were, by) reason of their, greater responsibilities, therefore, more to blame. It had been truly said that "Had the general laws been fully carried out in the spirit which prompted their adoption there would have been but little need for further legislation." A careful perusal of what may be fairly termed "an elaborate', code of laws and regulations," would, he believed, lead to the conviction that a fearless application of them would to-day have found the society in a much more satisfactory position, financially and otherwise. (Hear, hear.) Playing for Position.

That naturally led to the question, how could they improve their position in this respect? He ventured to answer: "By a more rigid disci- pline," by-which, he contended,greater efficiency would be attained. Many Qf their failures were due to hesitancy on the part of their executive bodies to administer even their existing laws with the necessary courage and firmness. •

===== 0002601_19160401_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
More Funds Fewer Members.

Of the various problems affecting the future of the Order the most dominant was, perhaps, that of lapses, and while it might be assumed that :they benefited financially thereby, it was. neither desirable nor creditable, that, as a society, they should be content: with such an. experience. Looking back ..for a moment they found that in the year 1886 there were 4049 branches with a membership of 585,279, and funds ,amounting to £2,839,715. In 1903 there were 3,933 branches with a membership of, 671,009 and funds amounting to £5,434,283. There, in .17 years' experience, whilst recording a• de- creaSe of 116 branches, ,there , was shown; an in- crease in members of 85,730, and a remarkable increase in funds amounting to £2,594,568-, or nearly double. But in 1911 there •were. only 3,725 branches and 601,691. members. The funds, however, totalled £6,805,408. There, in eight years, they again recorded a decrease of 208 branches and a decreaSe in membership of 69,318. Whilst deploring that great decrease in members in eight .years, it was pleasing to note, in the same period, the - very satisfactory in- crease in funds. Excepting in 1912, which recorded an increase in numbers, they had to regret decreases right up to date, though at the same time there had been a considerable increase ,financially. Many of their experien ed leaders had from time to time endeawmred- to explain the cause of that decline in membership, and it was evident that the main cause was through the adoption by the various trade unions, at differ- ent periods, of the payment of sick benefits, and, later, since the inception of the National

Insurance Act, the taking over, for •the. same benefits, by the same and other unions, the people, who otherwise would have joined the friendly societies. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19160401_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
Subject to Severe Competition.

• They could not rightly complain of that, for, to take 'a common-sense view, it was rather an advantage, and certainly attractive, to the young trade unionist to take his 'benefits through the society which controlled, so to speak, his bread and butter. And it would seem that the success of their society, during its early history, was largely due to the fact that no other channels for sick and funeral benefits but those of the friendly societies were open to the working classes. • So that, every- thing considered, it was hardly reasonable to imagine, in face. of the very severe competition surrounding them, the possibility of maintain- ing the numerical _ position prevailing in previous years. Another question affecting the future was their excessive sickness experience. Opinions differed as to the cause, whilst many affirmed that dual benefits, and workmen's ! compensation, were responsible. There could be no doubt that that was largely the case. He had an instance in mind where it was due entirely to long periods of reduced sick pay, or what, perhaps, might be more properly termed superannuation, for which nothing was paid, This was a part of their work which called for careful control, and was often responsible for serious depletion of funds, felt more acutely by the small branch. Valuers told them that their scales of contribution's were calculated to cover liabilities for sickness proper, as distinct from senility or old age. The difficulty was to differ- entiate, and it would seem that the one implied the other, and the other both. The consolida- tion of their sick funds, on the same lines as district funeral funds, suggested there, at any rate, a partial remedy, for whilst it would not remove the liability, it would be distributed over a 'greater area, and could be made more constant. Such a system, called the equalised system, was operated by a sister society, and was. he believed, in active operation in the Sheffield district of their Order.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
Amalgamating Small Branches.

The amalgamation of ..small branches was a question • which ;the future would demand, and Which had. :for - some time been rightly advocated. Some would even make it • com- pulsory in certain .circumstances. Compulsion .was just now rather an ugly word; but if they took their Minds back to the reforms of the ' past thirty years they would find, in every stage of life compulsory measures, which had proved to be absolutely necessary for the common good. (Hear, hear.) They 'should realise-- and they were so hard up against the fact that they must realise—that failing the revival of that voluntary spirit which achieved so much in the past, they would be faced •by the neces- sity of imposing drastic reforms', of a com- pulsory character. In conclusion, -Bro. Hip-

kiss declared that it would be well for the-nt to broaden their outlook generally, not from the point of view of the welfare of some indi- vidual branch or district, or even of the Order, or yet the success of friendly societies in general, but for the wellbeing of the community at large. They ought even to be content to

sacrifice some of their well-cherished ideas and principles, if by so doing they could, even in the least degree, satisfy 'themselves that by such sacrifice they could contribute to the generai good. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19160401_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
MEN WHO WERE HARD-UP.

Bro. Enoch Farrow, P.H.C.R., and a member of the Executive Council, congratulated Bro. Hipkiss on the thoughtful paper he had given. Many weak spots had been touched. There was no doubt that had they been more rigid-in their discipline the society would have made more progress, for certain branches had not taken the advice or sought the assistance of • the Central Authority, who knew how to handle , things better than anyone else. The question of lapses was a serious one. It seemed to have ' grown since they had made extensive use of the post office. Instead of intimating arrears by correspondence they ought to have kept to the old-time visitor, who, on going round, told members what they owed, and if it were a. time of adversity they could recommend to the other courts as to what should be done with them. Through the, post a man did not care about con- fessing that he was hard up. but if a district visitor went to him he would probably speak his mind, and in that way the visitor could be of great assistance to him. Whilst they did not 'like to hear of branches being struck off the roll, there were some branches which were certainly of no credit to the society, and where possible these should be amalgamated with other societies. At the next High Court meet- ing the society was to be divided into provinces. and he felt that they ought to have greater power over branches than they had had in the past. Another factor which had affected them was the excessive sickness, due greatly to the introduction of the Workmen's Compensation Act. He believed that the money which mem- bers derived from this Act, and also from other friendly ,societies,. had encouraged men' to stay away longer.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
WHEN THE LAPSES BEGAN.

Bro. -John Harris, of Chester, thought the greater number of the members they had lost followed the publication in the. " Standard " newspaper of the articles in which it was pointed out that the Foresters were only worth his. 9d. in the C. Of !course at the same. time they pointed out the financial deficiencies of other societies. • As a consequence of this their own London Executive Council brought forward a series of proposals for improvement, but by their vacillating methods they had not arrested the decrease in membership. After the article.; had been published they first decided that every court should be worth 15s. in the C. Thousand" of members left them, but others remain•ad loyal, and made their courts 1113 to 15s. in the C. Immediately that had been done the Expeu- Jive Council said they must make their courts I worth 17s. od. in the When that had been done they said they must make them worth 20s n the C. In his opinion, if they had said af. the outset they must be worth 20s. in the they might certainly have lost some members. I • , hut they would not have lost as many as they I had done by the methods they had adopted.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
ISOLATED COURTS.

Bro. Wilbraham was not so sure it would i.e wise to abolish country courts. They would have to. be very, careful in this respect. For instance, if the small court they had in his district at Caer Gwrle, Flints, were terminate I he was afraid members might lapse. The nearest they had to that was seven miles away, whereas in the towns they had courts in almo3t every • street. (Laughter, and hear, hear.) Bro. Whiteside (of Manchester) declared thal unless they stirred themselves up in connection with Juvenile Courts they . would go on dwindling away. The best thing was to get the !children in a friendly society while they were young, and, then, if they did not see a decrzms.! right away it would certainly pull up the de- crease. But, in any ease, there was no doula that, so far as Foresters were concerned, they were "not dead yet." (Laughter, and ap- plause.) Bro. Harry Summer was of opinion that although there had been a good deal of sickno!-. pay in recent years, they would profit by eventually, for men were now returning to work ciuite wAI, whereas before, when .there was little money coming into the. homes, they returned to work too, soon, and conAequently became ill again.

WARRINGTON'S FINE JUVENILE COURT.

John Latham, Of Earle-town, in seconding,.said they looked upon Warrington as having one of the finest • juvenile brandies in tie country.. It was well .managed, and the percentage of members, according to popula- tion, would bear comparison with any district in the Order. (Applause.) .In his opinion their greatest competitors were the trade unions. Children could be brought -up in the ideals and ways of friendly societies, and yet within it week of being in the workshop the influence of the past was lost. , The proposition was carried heart y. It was decided that in future the. FederatiOn and the Juvenile Federal Council,;should meet together frequently, with a view to co-ordi- nating their work.

CLEANLINESS OF THE BIROS.

Poultry suffer from parasites, both internal and external. As in all cases of trouble of this -kind, says the "Poultry World," prevention is Letter than cure. Internal parasites should be guarded -against by .givin4-n pineli Of salt in. all soft food, and further by administering _Epsom- salts- once a nionth to all birds. The correct quantity is two teaspoonfuls to a 'pen of six adult fowls. - For chickens the quantity must be reduced. Young chicks reqiiire- a. tea- spoonful to twenty, and for elder ones..inerease the amount proportionately. . Epson' salts should be given at night, and the dropping trays changed next morning, the dirty ones being cleansed. External parasites should be guarded against by the provision .of sufficient dusting material otaTE dry and very fine, mut by going over the birds with insect powder as often as possible. A counsel of perfection is to give a dust around the vent and under each wing on re- leasing a bird from the trap-nest- In ,summer, once a fortnight is Certainly not -too often to .treat all birds; and this is best done after they have gone to roost, as then the powder remains. in the fluff all night. Washing birds, though often practised, is not necessary for utility foivii. They •have no sweat glands. A warning should, be given here. A paraffin emulsion is a dangerous wash for fowls: indeed, paraffin ap- pears, tO'be surprisingly severe in its effect many of ..our - domesticated

When this was . done no birds were allowed upon it till the next season, and then it started with the young early broods.—" Feathered World."

===== 0002601_19160401_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
WHERE TO SET HENS.

If hens are disturbed while they are sitting they are much less likely to bring off good brood's. The place chosen, therefore; for sitting hens should be quiet, as :if they can see the othCr7birds, Or be disturbed by' them, .they are liable tO:become restless. A special shed should be-devoted to them, which hould be well venti- lated and fairly dark. A spadeful of. earth EafoUld be Placed in the nest box, and it should be hollowed out in the centre in the form of a saucer, so .that no eggs May be rolled into the corners and so become chilled. On the earth there 'should be placed a little straw— oat preferably—which should be well :rubbed. in the hands to make it softer. If an. egg, gets broken the straw should be renewed, as a dirty, evil-smelling nest has a bad effect 'upon the hatching results.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
A GOOD SITTING BOX.

1 This is a necessary factor towards successfull hatching results. The nest box must be so large as to albw. the hen freedom 'of action, and to sit low down, spreading herself well overl the nest, as the most reliable sitters invariably , do. The box should be at least* 16 inches square; in fact, it. is better to have it 18 inches. The box should be constructed with- out a bottom, so that moisture can be more readily secured than is possible if a fixed bot- tom is part of the appliance. With a bottom- less box also cleanliness is facilitated. When the poultry-keeper is troubled With rats, which frequently have the temerity to burrow under the nest and remove the eggs, it is a good plan to fix fine mesh wire-netting underneath the box, which will be found effectually to piotect,, the hen and her eggs., It is also highly impor- • • taut that the sitting box shall be so constructed that Nish air can readily enter. It is often imagined that as the box is usually placed out in the open, and the joints are by no means air-tight, all the necessary fresh air can Zie secured. The importance of a constant supply' of fresh air is very often overlooked. Want of a proper supply, or a supply that comes through filthy 'surroundings, is often a cause of " dead-

===== 0002601_19160401_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EGGS MUST HAVE MOISTURE.

An insufficient supply of moisture is a very common cause of .unsuccessfill hatching results, for in this 'case the two thin skins immediately inside the shell become thick and tough, giving the chicken great difficulty to pierce. Very often the eggs are damped with a wet cloth, but. a plan that I have found to give better results is to water the earth around the out- side of the nest box two or three times a wt-k. The heat of the hen's body attracts the noois- ..ture through the earth within the box, throqgh the straw, and so to the eggs. On the (!ay Previous to hatching it is a good plan to dip the eggs for live or ten seconds into water heatq to 102 deg: Fahr., which has the effect of Soften- ing these two thin skins, and so making the exit of the chicken somewhat easier. Cooling the eggs should take place once a day, the time depending upon the outside temperature On 'a cold day ten minutes is quite sufficient, whereas during warm weather • half an hour- 's none too long. The daily cooling is for the sake of the embryo chickens within the shells, and not so. much for the sake of the hen. It is found that Without daily airing the chickens Make their appearance sooner, but they are less hardy, and, involve considerable difficulty to rear.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0048.txt | included=true | included =====
DAILY COOLING.

Daily Cooling of the eggs is essential in order to 'strengthen and stimulate the embryo chickens. The broody hen is fed once a day, and this is the time when the eggs are cooled. This should be of ten minutes' duration during the first week, increasing to a quarter of an hour as the process of incubation advances. Cooling need not be extended to the same length of time as when the eggs are in an incubator, • since, under the latter conditions, cooling takes place in a room where they are not exposed. to outside air. In an incubator the eggs are turned twice every day. Turning is equally necessary when the eggs are under -hens; but the hen herself performs this duty. Were eggs to remain in the ilea or in an incu- bator, during the whole process without being turned, it is very'. doubtful -indeed if any chickens would hatch. If, however, some did actually emerge from the shell many of them would .be very badly deformed, owing to their having lain in one position.—" Farm and Home."

===== 0002601_19160401_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
SKIN RAW WITH ECZEMA.

Back To Duty—Thanks To Zam-Buk.

" Whilst serving on H.M.S. Canopus," writes .Chief Petty Officer R. J. Pulvertaft, "I

knocked a bit of skin off my leg, and a bad form of eczema set in. The skin became raw and inflamed, and was intensely irritable. I

was prevented from doing my duties, and was sent home to a Naval Hospital. During the voyage to England 1 was treated as a 'sot ease.'

"My leg-was the colour of raw beef, and was covered with running sores, the pain of which' was well-nigh unbearable. After a period of hospital tseatment I was sent to my home, 25, 'Clifford-terrace; Budeaux,,Devonport. Here I stayed for some time, still suffering from the awful liores .that _broke out also on my neck, 'face, lips, ear, - and mouth.. My hands were also attacked. Life became -a •burden. I tried another hospital, also several doctors, and a 'number of home remedies. My case, however, was a., chronic one. The itching prevented Bleep, and drove me nearly wild. • "One day, my wife heard about a neighbour's .child being •completely cured by Zam-Buk of an obstinate attack of eczema, so, On her advice, I began to use this balm. Zam-Buk at once gave relief by soothing the intolerable irritation. The sores lost their angry appear- ance, and 'ceased to discharge. So evident .was the improvement that a doctor, who had attended me, remarked that I was making splendid progress. Through keeping on with Zam-Buk the distressing eczema was soon entirely rooted out." Petty Officer Pulvertaft's remarks were borne out by his Wife, Who added that when her hus- band was called up as a Reservist at the out- break of war, the first thing he asked for to 'pack in his kit was a box of Zam-Buk.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, APRIL 1, 1916.

FARM AND GARDEN.

BREAKING THE SULKING HABIT IN HORSES.

A reader of the "Breeders' Gazette ". says : " I find in my 30 years' practical experience that in Correcting the sulking habit the best way is to have a bucket of water and a cup at hand. Pour some .water in the horse's ears. If that does not raise him, pour it in his nose with a dash, to make him think you are going to drown him. This has worked on horses that had the .habit for years. One or two doses will suffice."

===== 0002601_19160401_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
PREVENTING COLLAR SORES.

For years I have been washing horses' shoulders with salt brine and other liquid mix- tures and achieving only indifferent results in preventing collar sores. Last year a man of wide experience in handling workhorses on the farm taught me how to secure immunity from sore shoulders, and the method worked per- fectly. I pass it on:—" Look well to the fit of, the collar. Clip the long, 'ougli hair so as to secure a smooth, even suilaCe for the collar to rest upon. Each evening after unharnessing. have a bucket filled with common air-slacked lime handy. Dust the shoulder thoroughly With it. Brush it out the next morning with a soft brush." This is simple, inexpensive, and practical. It prevents collar sores. The lime seems to have about the same effect as talcum powder on a child's skin. A whole string of horses may be treated while one was being washed in the old way. The only additional advice is to keep the collars clean.—" Breeders' Gazette."

===== 0002601_19160401_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GREAT BOOM IN PIGS.

In the first ten weeks of the year 7,000 fewer fat pigs came to market than the average sup- plies and 6,000 less. stores. This fact in itself may not mean much, but, taken in conjunction with the record prices now ruling, it appears to indicate a shortage of stocks, and forbids the hope of lower prices for consumers. The situa- tion is unique. Farmers never complained if bacon pigs made 10s. per score, and they are now making 175., and yet it is asserted that there is no profit attached to feeding them! It is true that barley meal costs double the usual price, but feeding peas are quoted in London market at 555. per 5041 b., and these, with other foods, are much cheaper than. barley. Person- ally, I think, with good management, and especially where separated milk is available, bacon and pork can be produced at a good profit even at the pretnt price of feeding stuffs. Certainly, if the cannot, neither will it pay where food is at the lowest, with bacon at 9s. per score. The fact is the British farmer is very half-hearted in the business of pig production. We are, as a nation, among the very smallest producers per head of our population of any of :the civilised countries, and the German ratio is seven times as great as ours.

Something should be clone to encourage our labourers to keep pigs. For one thing, the absurd regulation against keeping them within a certain distance of a dwelling-house ought to be modified. Pigs will doubtless be the chief live stock of the small holder, and through him the coming years will probably see a large in- crease in the country's total stocks.—" Farm and Home."

===== 0002601_19160401_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
CINEMAS FOR THE VILLAGES.

It was scarcely possible to devote too much attention to the social amenities of village life. lle believed it was a profound mistake to sup- pose that young people left the country for town life because they could get more wages, though that influenced clever boys and girls; it was more a question of amusement than wages. Though his opinion might shock some people, he believed the introduction of cinema shows into villages was an excellent social improve- ment. If the recommendation of the committee materialised, he thought he 'might promise that this aspect of country life would not be forgot- ten. The question of subsidiary village indus- tries was important, and in this connection the success of the Rural League had been astonish- ing in employing industry in villages for sup- plying goods .fornierly imported from Germany and Austria.—Lord Selborne.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

THREE GOOD ANNUALS

...The first is the Portulaca, which I think is the prettiest annual I know. It does best in a very hot,, dry situation, and the more sun it gets the more effective it is. Sow out of doors in April, and later to keep Up a succession of bloom.. In a very wet season the Portulaca is not a success. Another easily grown annual I very fond of . is Love-in-a-Mist (Nigella). It is also known as Fennel Flower, from the resemblance of the leaves to those of this well- known herb. The flowers are of .a pretty blue, and are very useful for cutting, as they last in trood condition for some time. There is no more ,dainty accompaniment to the flowers when cut than their own leafage. This plant may be sown very thinly in clumps where it is to re- main. in the early days Of April. My . third lnnual is the ,Night Scented Stock (Matthibla bieornis). The blooms during the day are very rlowdy, but towards the evening they open, and the .scent is. then veryi pleasing.. The seeds, which are very minute, must be sown very thinly in soil which has been broken down yory fine. If sown too thickly the plants seem -1-o damp off very soon.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0055.txt | included=true | included =====
SWEET WILLiAMS.

Few hardy flowers better repay any care spent on then' than these, and they should be found in more or less quantity in every garden. Those who have never seen the Sweet William at its best can have ,but a faint idea what a glorious effect large, well-grown plants produce, the colours ranging from the richest maroon to pure white, the flowers finely and distinctly marked, the eyes in many of the blooms being very telling. What renders the Sweet William of much value to the amateur is the fact that it can be raised from seed without the aid of glass. Sow the seed in April in a well-prepared bed of soil in a sunny situation, and thin out the young plants, transferring them to some other part of the garden until the time comes to place them in their permanent quarters. About the lattev part of September transplant them to where they are to flower, and the fol- lowing, summer, if . all goes well, they will flower freely and compensate the grower for any trouble he has taken.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====
CUTTING POTATO SETS.

Some people still cut their potato sets instead of planting the whole tuber. The best time to do this is about three days before planting, so that the severed parts may get dry and hardened before planting. If. cut too long before planting I have found that. the sets axeiitble to..shrivel. In the-case of long kidney .shaped tubers. I prefer cutting them through the middle from the eye and downwards, and when .planting :laying them flat on the cut. side. If the tubers are, say, from 3oz. to 4oz. each in Weight they will make two good :sets, while tile still larger tubers. may give three and four sets if a strong eyels-cut with each.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0057.txt | included=true | included =====
DWARF PEAS.

I do not advise anyone to grow these under the impression that they are as profitable as the tall-growing sorts, because this is not the ease. Circumstances, however, alter cases, and there are two classes of amateurs to whom the dwarf peas should appeal, viz., those whose gardens are small and those who find it difficult to get pea sticks tall enough -to support the. varieties that grow to a height of :1 feet or 5 feet..

===== 0002601_19160401_art0058.txt | included=true | included =====
SCOTCH KALE.

This is one of the hardiest of winter greens, and about the last to tun to seed. Plants from seed sown early in May and put out after the earliest potatoes have been cleared will have made good sturdy plants by the time Winter sets in. • The more exposed atkd hardier they are grown the better able will thy be to stand the rigours of the winter. If the heads are cut off towards the latter part of ,the winter the stems will become furnished with side sprouts, which are among•the most delicious of all the Brassica tribe, and which ,will continue to be produced without running to seed as long as they are in request. When spring cabbages get plentiful they are then preferred, and the .kales may he .PulledittP and the ground got ready for another. erop.-.-Wiek, in "Farm and Home.'-'.

Moreover, the exclusion of edu- cated women from the higher departments of our textile factories is a loss 'to British trade, for our productions suffer in taste and charm thereby. British Flannelette.

The vogue for silk, predicted some time back, flannelette question, for the Army authorities and the British Red Cross Society have sub- jected this popular and useful material to very severe tests. The result is .extremely gratifying to British manufacturers of the better qualities, who have lately formed an association to protect their industry, which they say is suffering from the ignorance of the public as to the real facts. British. flannelette is made from specially pre- pared yarn, closely woven, its surface being slightly teased to give it a soft nap. This material cannot be sold in peace times for less than sixpence per yard. The cheaper makes come from the Continent, and are retailed at half that price. These are highly inflammable. and as they come chiefly from enemy countries their importation should be forbidden. Pur- chasers can easily protect themselves by insist- ing on being sunplied with goods bearing the name of a reputable British firm, These .are no more inflammable than ordinary calico. Silk the Summer Fabric.

The rage for silk, predicted some time back, is now an assured feature of summer wear, and no other material, velvet alone excepted, is so becoming to women of all ages. It is diist resisting and not necessarily extravagant, since it can be employed in the simplest styles, and yet look smart. Faille, though thick, is very soft,and drapes gracefully, and will prove a rival to taffetas, whilst some of the newest tailored suits are of blue or black poult, a silk that bears a striking resemblance to taffetas. Ribbed and corded silks will enjoy a record vogue, nor are the quaintly patterned and figured silks that charmed former generations neglected. There are old-world chine pompadours, and wohderful Watteau brocades, the latter promising-a note of dignity in summer modes that has been lacking for a considerable time. Taffetas in black and coloured grounds has showers of gold and silver roses lightly scattered over the surface of the f',hric, whilst plain black is relieved by much jet or diamente.

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIL.

Royal Hospitality.

The tea parties given by die King and Queen at tlie Royal Riding School to wounded soldiers were greatly enjoyed by some three thousan I guests. Throughout last summer parties of wounded men took tea, by their Majesties' invi- tation, in the 'gardens of Buckingham Palace. and in all prbbability similar hospitality will be extended to the soldiers in the coming months. The guests are divided into groups, and a ' member of the Royal Family presides over each set of attendants. At the Bucking- ham Palace festivities last- week a King, three Queens, a Prince, and half a dozen Princesses all assisted in putting the guests quite at their ease, and such is the general air of homeliness on these occasions that no one ventures to in- vent those familiar stories of royal- condescen, sion which at other times would not be spared us.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0061.txt | included=true | included =====
The Lack of Business Wcimen.

In some of the profession, the theatrical especially, it is the usual thing for the daughter to take up her father's calling, but it rarely happens that the proprietor of a business or factory takes his daughters into his office. More often than not it is his chief concern to keep the feminine members of his family re- moved as far as possible from all contact with "the shop," or the business. The war, how- ever, has brought about a change here, as else- where, and as is the case in France, the wives and daughters of many heads of wholesale and retail commercial concerns are being called upon to assist in the business because the sons have been drafted into the Army. The change will be all the better 'if it proves to be of a permanent character. There are far too few eductAted women in business, as is proved by the appointment of men with trade experienee to guard the interests of women employed in munition and other factories. There is also a lack of women qualified to sit on committees and councils dealing with economic war problems. The few women members of treding firms and of women manufacturers, who have

GOOD LUCK TO UNCLE NED !

MY DEAR CHILDREN, I have had a splendid lot of letters this week to be forwarded to Uncle Ned. They will be sent ,at once, so that, he will be able to see all the nice things that have been said of him. In most of them are the same expressions of good luck, cif praise for his not having shirked his „duty, of pleasure at the increasing mem- bership of the Corner, and the hope that he will soon be able to come home to take charge of his big family of nephews and nieces. I have been able to reproduce only extracts from these letters and have naturally not printed the same ideas over and over again. You must ,not ,t4ink ,those ideas are not ..appre- ciated. They are, only there is not room for repetition in "The Examiner." But Uncle Ned will see them all—and perhaps he will say what he thinks of them.

A BIITER DISAPPOINTMENT.

How The War Has Affected Christians. Despite the fact that. oelic.,ve in the righteousness of our cause. be Rev. T. T. James stated, in his sermon at Wyel'ffo Conuregational Church on Sunday evening, that war had come, as a bitter disappointhiei r st, drib. "Say what we will," he said, "as to the righteousness of our own cause in this conflict, and be persuaded as we' may that any other step, either in fairness to oppressed nations or in defence of our own homes and loved ones, was absolutely impossible. and free our minds as we may of every u-wnrthy motive and passion and purpose—a difficult enqugh thing to do in the light of some of our national experi- ences—there is no truly Christian man or woman to whom this war does not come as a bitter disappointment I': TYIII-f a rl;san- pointment that after we have gazed for nearly two thousand years upon that sad figure on the Cross. a cockpit where men seek each other's lives. It is more than a disappoi7-1 , • ~ de. erate and colossal tragedy. Germany's So-called Culture.

WYCLIFFE P.S.A.

There was a very good meeting on Sunday,, presided over by the Rev. T. T. James, Rev. P. meson gave the address upon "What is the Church's Mission during this war?" He said he was glad there was a'growing desire for an amalgamation of the .church's work, but what wai the church's mission at this time? Some asketl, "Why keep churches open while the war was on?" Christ came primarily to establish righteousness—yea, righteousne7s before peace. A conflict must come first. Some asked "Why could we not love our enemies even while the war raged?" Severity often meant love to help. So with our enemies wrong must be punished. Peace with dishonovr was worse than defeat. We must be true to the great trust given us—do and be right, working for right- eousness. Spiritual power behind the churches kept the churches open. We Must prosecute the war for the right, not only by material forces, 'and that involved the greater need for the churches to be open. Mrs. Moore sang effectively "Rock of Ages" and "The Beautiful Land. Mr. Cotton took part in the meeting. Mr. Payton presided at the organ. Two- strangers and soldiers received hearty welcomes in our meeting. A vote of sympathy was passed to Bro. J. Brown's widow and family in the loss by death of the husband and father.

HoME NOTES.

Profitable Recipes And Useful

Suggestions.

Ham Fritters.—A nice change from fried or broiled ham for breakfast is to take slices of cold boiled ham, and dust a little pepper over them. Make a batter of a cupful of milk, 2 eggs, niup- ful of flour prepared; dip the slices of ham in the batter, and fry them in boiling lard. Drain from the fat, and serve the shoes on a hot.plat- ter. Cold boiled pork may be prepared-the-same way.

Toad-in-the-hole.--Cut cold meat into dice and put in a greased baking dish. Beat 1 egg light, add to it 1 pint of milk;, stir into it gradually 6 tablespoonfuls of flour, beating stiffly all the time. Strain through a fine sieve; add half a teaspoonful of salt and a little pepper. Bake one hour. Serve in a baking-dish.

Beef Stewed with Onions.—Cut .some tender beef in small pieces, and season it with pepper and salt, slice some onions and add to it, with water enough in the stewpan to make a gravy; let it stew slowly till the beef is thoroughly done, then add some pieces of butter rolled in flour to make a rich gravy. Cold beef may be done in the same way, only the onions must be stewed first and the meat added. If the water should stew away too much, put in a little more.

Stewed Celery.—Scrape and wash celery stalks clean. Cut in pieces and soak in cold water for fifteen minutes; put them in a saucepan of boil- ing water with a teaspoonful of salt and boil thirty minutes; when done, drain and throw in cold water. • Put a tablespoonful of butter in a small pan, let it melt and add one tablespoonful of flour, •miz until smooth; add half a pint of milk and stir until it boils; add three table- spoonfuls of the eelery water with a- little salt and pepper; pony over the celery, stir over the fire five minutes and serve.

Too Muth, Tea.—The excessive drinking of tea is responsible for a good deal of the ancemia that exists among girls. They have tea in the morn- ing, tea in the afternoon, and tea at night, with two or three extra cups thrown in. It is bad to keep the teapot on the hob, filling it up with water without changing the leaves. All the goodness is soon drawn from the tea, and the later cups contain nothing but tannin and a little colouring matter. Strong tea is very constipating. Tea should never be taken with meat. There is no nourishment in tea. Tea should never be given to growing children.

Onions au fromage- (German).—Peel and wash three or four large onions, and cut them into slices inch in thickness. Spread a baking- tin with butter, place the onion slices side by side upon it, season them with salt and pepper, and bake them in a hot oven until they are tender. Have ready some grated Parmesan cheese, sprinkle the onions rather plentifully with it, and return them to the oven for a few minutes for the cheese to melt, when they must be served upon circles of toast spread with mustard butter.

Beetroots a la creme (French).—Boil, peel, and cut into dice three beetroots. Quarter, peel, and slice two Spanish onions, and fry them till tender in butter without allowing them to colour. Put iqZ. of butter into a saucepan and when it has melted stir into it smoothly a tablespoonful of flour; cook for a minute or two, then dilute gradually with half a pint of cream (or half a pint of milk beaten with the yolk of an egg), and season with. salt, pepper, and two tablespoonfuls of powdered sugar dissolved in three tablespoonfuls of vinegar; add the beet- roots and onions, and make thoroughly tot, but without' boiling, or the sauce may curdle.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
CARRY YOUR OWN PARCELS.

In these days when all active patriotic carters have joined the Artillery, or other sections of the Army in which their knowledge of horses will be useful to their country, and all the big boys are flocking to the Colours and errand boys are taking their places at the works, every' consideration should be shown their formek civil employers. . Tradesmen, who have a de- pleted staff and are yet expected to deliver goods purchased are put to a great and unnecessary amount of trouble. We can at least help the country by carrying our own parcels. Up to the eve of the war few peoiile had the courage—if it requires any—to carry home their own bundles. Indeed many people have a con- temptible fear of being seen to carry any parcel, however small, having the absurd idea that there is social degradation in the act. The most trifling as well as weighty packages must be sent home to them, no matter how much to the inconvenience of others. • This arises from a low sort of pride. There is a feeling that is higher—that arises from a consciousness oi there being something in the individual not to be affected by such accidents—worth and weight of character, This latter feeling was exhibited, 'by the son of Jerome Napoleon Bonaparte. While in College he was one day carrying a broom he had just purchased, when he met a friend, who, noticing the broom with surprise, exclaimed : "Gracious, why did you not have it sent home?" The sensible 'reply of young Bonaparte was: "I am not ashamed to carry home anything which belongs to me,"

THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, APRIL 1, 1916.

STOCKTON HEATH AND WARRINGTON.

Three years ago the Stockton Heath Parish Council were discussing the question of amalgamation with Warringten. Mr. H. J. Westbrook on that occasion described the suburb in which he resides as a coy damsel .—a'personage that was being wooed by War- rington, which, so far as we can .remember, he did not favour with a corresponding description. On that occasion, however, the price of gas, electricity, and water, and the question of tram services were the subjects entered upon—subjects which showed plainly enough the extent to which the damsel relies upon the town for her conveniences. This week it has been made apparent that War- rington's aid is needed in another direction. The disposal of refuse has evidently become a great burden at Stockton Heath, and the Corporation . has been approached to lend a helping hand. The difficulties are so great that Mr. F. Pearson, at the annual parish meeting this week, suggested that they would have to get out of the difficulties by being 'incorporated with Warrington. The present is rather a bad time to talk of rearranging areas, but clearly something of a practical -kind should be started at once. At all events, Stockton Heath has lost the character of/ a coy damsel when she comes to War- rington to ask us to assist her to dispose of her refuse.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
AGITATORS ON THE CLYDE.

It was announced in the House of Commons on Tuesday that, on the previous Friday, at the request of the Minister of Munitions, six ringleaders of a strike movement on the Clyde had been removed from the district by the military authorities. A self-appointed body had adopted a policy of holding up the production of important munitions, with the object'of compelling the Government to repeal the Military Service Act and the Munitions Of War Act. They had succeeded in bringing out on strike certain workmen engaged in the construction of heavy guns. The details of this mischievous and anti-national agitation made a very painful inigtession on the House. It is, however, absolutEty clear that the move- ment is altogether outside of and alien to trade unionism, and the influence ,of trade union leaders has been exerted loyally to prevent strikes during the war. The Govern- ment will have the country' behind them in,. •toy action they may take to put an end to this disgraceful attempt to hold up znuni- -.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ALLIES' CONFERENCE.

The Conference of the Allies, sitting at Paris, have adopted a series of resolutions which embody their decision to establish and maintain complete unity of military, diplo- matic and economic action. They propose to take common action without delay- to dis- tribute equally the burden of maritime trans- port and stop the rise in freights, and to establish a permanent committee, in ,Paris, representing all the Allis, to strengthen and co-ordinate the measures taken to prevent supplies from reaching the enemy. Thc people who write and talk as if this country only needed a " strong " Government to win the war offoits own bat, and as if every prob- lem of the war could be considered from a purely British point of view, would do well to try and think out for themselves the significance of. the -Conference in all its beer-

Bold Street Wesleyans Raise £67 For

===== 0002601_19160401_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
NURSE BLIKEMORE.

Death And Funeral Of Well-known

Social Worker.

In the death, on Monday last, of Miss Hannah Blakemore, a highly-respected member of the nursing proTeßsiOn has passed away. She was 61 years a age, and throughout her life, by her professional and social work, did much to relieve suffering. She was born at Donnington, Salop, and came to Warrington in 1882. Five years later she became foster-mother and in- dustrial trainer at the Cottage Homes at Pad- gate, and on her retirement in 1890 received the testimony of the Board of Guardians in a letter which stated that "the official and personal conduct of Miss Blakemore has in all respects been highly exemplary."- From 1891 to 1898 she was connected with the Macalpine Hospital, Manchester, being at first matron of the, Rescue Home and for several years matron of the Maternity Home. For reasons of health she went to South Africa in 1898, and was there during the whole-course of the Boer war. She engaged in general and special nursing in Cape .Colony, Transvaal, Natal, the Orange Free State, and Zululand.. AMong the natives in the last-named State she did much useful work. In 1910 She returned to this-country and identi- fied herself with the Warrington Guild of Help, the secretary of which, in a letter to her tele.- tivqs, -'has,; stated, that "she, did most' useful service .fer .the ouild .when her, health was better, and T shall not forget the spirit in which she did 'it.- She worshipped at BeWsey-road Wesleyan-Church, and on hearing of her death the Revs Thoriurs Miller, i former Minister wrote that "She was a splendid Christian: Like later another she' Was a loyal Methodist and a most generous supportefcif our church." Nurse Blakemore died at the Royal Infirmary, Liver- pool, from chronic bronchitis and cardiac failure.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, APRIL 1, 1916.

THE, INFIRMARY.

Splendid Work Performed Under Difficulties.

MORE MONEY NEEDED.

The Governors of the Warrington Infirmary and Dispensary held their annual meeting at the Town Hall, on Thursday afternoon, Mr. J. H. Smethurst (chairman of the Board of Man- agement) presiding ia ogri -of the President (Sir Gilbert Greenall. Bart.). who, in apologis- ing for his absence, wrote:— . "It is most- .satisfactory to note the great amount of Work that has been done by the institution during the last twelve months, not- withstanding the • difficulties with which we have had to contend. Something will undoubt- edly have to be done to meet the increasing' cost of maintenance. Our income ha a increased, but at nothing like the ratio to which the en-1 penditure has done, and is likely to do in the future. It is most gratifying to see the Work- ing :Men's Committee have been able to raise such a handsome amount, considering how 1 many of their supporters are ,away .fighting. Our institution his -done ‘everything:,:vossible" to relieve the wounded. and I feel this la appreciated on all hands. We must all regret that Alderman Burton has retired from the. chairmanship of the Working Men's Cotniuit- tee, after forty years' strenuous work, for which the whole town owes him a great debt of gratitude. I am sure we are ail delighted to hear of the honour to be conferred upon Colonel Crosfield, one of our best supporters, and we congratulate not only him, but his regiment and the town." The Chairman formally Moved that Lieut.- Colonel Crosfield, D. 5.0., be congratulated upon the honours conferred upon him, and the pro- posal was agreed to with enthusiasm.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
THE YEAR'S WORK AND ITS COST.

The Board of Management, in thait 106th annual report, stated that during 1915 the patients treated at the institution had been as follows :—ln-patients : Civil, 1,059. against 1,219 in 1914; military, 74, against 55; total, 1,133, against 1,274. Out-patients: General, 3,787, against 4,183; ophthalmia, 222, against 921; dental, 145, against .164; total, 4.154, against 5,268. Home patients: 1,300, against 1,067. Accidents. 5,535, against 4,868. Total, 12.122, against 12,477. Operations performed, 728, against 809; radio- grams taken, 419, against 429. An Increased Debit BalarlCC.

The year began with a debit balance of £1,219 45., and closed with a debit balance of £1,538 13s. 4d: The ordinary expenditure Nyas 16s. 10d., an increase of .£464 4s. The ordinary income, including £1,064 received for military patients, was £5,308 .7s, exl., an increase of £507 9s. 2d. Extraordinary income was £lO, transferred from the Special War Fund. The income froui investments amounted to £1,145 10s. 2d., a decrease of £2B 18s. Bd. The annual subscriptions amounted to £l,lll 25., adecrease of 16s. The amount received from the Congre- gational collections was £259 .14s. 5d., a very welcome increase of £l4 9s. 6d. The ,contribu- tion from the Working Men's Infirmary Com- mittee was £1,113 98. Bd., a decrease of £lO2 78. 9d. This committee collected £1,262 15s, 9d., which was divided as follows :—£1,113 9s. &L., to ordinary Infirmary account; £4O ,to Infirmary Special War Fund; and £lO9 6s. ld. to war charities. The Endowment Fund was increased by the receipt of a legacy of £lOO bequeathed by the late Mrs. Amy Kendall Whitley. The Samaritan Fund, which assists necessitous -patients to obtain surgical appli- ances, continued to be of great service. • Thanks of the Board.-

The Board tender to the T-ionorary Surgeons and the Honorary Dental Surgeon. their high appreciation of the valuable services rendered by them. The Board desire to express their grateful thanks to the Ladies'%lnfirmary Com- mittee; to the Hospital Sunday Committee; to the Working Men's Infirmary Committee; to the 'lnfirmary Ball Committee; to the ladies of the Children'a -Cot Committee; to the White Heather Day Committee; and to all donors of money or kind for their assistance. The Board are pleased to report that the annual Christmas treat to the patients and staff was again pro- vided without expense to the Hospital Funds. The Board express their appreciation of the efficient manner in which the stag have per- formed their respective duties.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
War's Effects.

The continuance of the war has naturally again caused very considerable extra expendi- ture for provisions, drugs, &c., and the closest possible supervision has been constantly exer- cised to provide efficiency with the least pos- sible extra cost. The Board are gratified to place on record the fact that several of the members of the Medical Board are away en active service, and it is sincerely hoped that they will return safely. It is impossible to fill the positions of the Resident House Sur- geons, owing to the calls of the Military Auth- orities, but the Board rely on. the Medical Board to provide as efficient service as pos3itoe under the difficult conditions, and it is to be hoped that subscribers will recognise that the needs of the patients are being attended to and continue to assist the instit%tion financially, The accommodation provided for military patients has not been utilised as much as had been expected, but the treatment of those placed under our care has resulted in most satisfactory recovery, not a single death having to be recorded.

How to Help the Doctors.

The report of the Medical Board gave details of the work of the institution as outlined in the general report. Regarding the minor acci- dents, which had again increased to 5,596, as against 4,868 last ~year, the report ,stated:— " These cases coming in at all tithes of the day and night are an increasing difficulty to the resident staff even in normal times, and an earnest appeal is .made that all trivial cases fihould when -possible, attend at the nearest doctor. The Medical Board are glad to record that residents and nursing staff have carried out their increased work with care and energy, and the comfort of the patients has been well maintained."

===== 0002601_19160401_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
SORELY-TRIED IN MANAGEMENT.

In moving the adoption of the report and balance sheet, the Chairman . remarked that everyone of them was aware of the position of the institution as a hospital. Like other in- stitutions throughout the country, we in War- rine,ton had been very sorely tried during the past year in many ways, but owing to the -Icyal. support of every member of the Bcard of Management, .the Medical Board, and the' staff, it had been possible to • meet their diffi- culties with a minimum of trouble and annoy- ance to all concerned. Their trials would be at once apparent when he mentioned that they had one of •their honorary surgeons at the Front doing his bit, their honorary pathologist .was away doing his share, and another hoa. surgeon was in °barge of another hospital. They had not 'been able •to fill the positions, either, of ...the. senipr, or junior .Surgeons. Every- thing had .been iii a state of transition, and if it had not 'been for the kindness of the Medical Board be. did ...not knsw whatever they would have done on the medical and surgical side. As far as regarded the patients dealt w ith in the Infirmary proper, they had practically no waiting list. • -

===== 0002601_19160401_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
Increase In Home-Patients.

There had been an increase in the number of patients in the home-patients' department. and they all ought to be guileful to• Dr. Man- son, the visiting home surgeon, for the way he had attended to those people, for, although his work during the past few years had increased by from 35 per cent. to 40 per cent., he Was not charging any more money for, his services. As regarded the dentistry department, this had been carried on as usual, and to Mr. lied- ley ,Taylor they tendered their sincere thanks. In dealing with the medical and surgical side of the hospital, one was very thankful to see that in the present acting house surgeon, Dr. Cruikshank, they had a gentleman upon whom they could rely to do his best. The reason he was not serving in the Army as because he had already been disabled in his country's service; having• lost a portion of his hand. and was, therefore, no longer eligible for the Army. His misfortune was, of course, their good for- tune. (Hear, hear.) They had commenced to develop in a very small way the idea of the paying in-patient as Aistinct from the paying . _ _

===== 0002601_19160401_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
EDUCATION COMMITTEE.

Re-Election Of Chairman "ind Vice-

Chairman.

At the annual meeting of the Warrington Education Committee on Monday night, the Director of Edueation presiding in the initial stages, Councillor Pemberton proposed that Mr. A. .T. Jolley he chairman Of the committee for the ensuing twelve months. Mr. Jolley took a keen interest in education and was well fitted for the duties of chairman, having been chairman of other •public bodies. Councillor Henry Roberts had held the position for three years, and he thought that these honours should go round, and that terms of even three years were too long. The average for the Mayors in War- rington was two years, though there had been exceptional circumstances that had resulted in the chair being held for a longer term.

There was no seconder, and Mr. E. Plinston moved the re-election of Councillor Henry Roberts. Councillor Roberts was a representative member and had served the authority exceedingly well. Mr. R: T. Fairclough seconded, and said that Councillor Roberts was an exceptionally good man; they could not do better than retain his services. . _ _

- The motion was carried unanimously. Councillor Roberts said he was greatly ob- liged for the renewal of their confidence. He would do his best to merit it. (Applause.) Alderman Bennett moved the re-election of Mr. Jolley as deputy-chairman. He Rad al- ready proved that they could not possibly have a better one.

Councillor Dickenson seconded and it was caxTied. _ _

Mr. Jolley thanked the committee for their expression of goodwill and expressed the hope that the good feeling that had prevailed among them for many years would continue for many years. (Hear, hear.)
