A SATISFACTORY YEAR.

At the 24th annual ordinary general meeting of the shareholders of Messrs. Fletcher, Russell and Co.; • Ltd., at Warrington,. on Wednesday, a satisfactory year's working in abnormal eon- ditions, caused by the war, was reflected in th 9 paying' of a dividend of five per cent. end a bonus of five per cent; the transference of £5,443 to the reserve fund, now standing at £24,306; the carrying forward of a balance of £5,928; and the substantial redemption of debenture loans. Mr. T. W. Fletcher presided, and all the directors were present.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
REDUCING THE DEBENTURES.

The Chairman, moving the adoption of the accounts and the directors' and auditors; reports, said they might consider the result of the year's working satisfactory, having regard to the abnormal conditions of the time. (Hear, hear.) There had been, a decrease in the ordi-. nary trade of the company, which had, how- ever, been more than made up by other special work. The special departments had been work- ing at full pressure. to keep pace with the de- mand for the firm's well-known specialities. Nearly 250 employes had joined His Majesty's Forces since \the commencement of the war; their names and the regiments they had joined were shown on the roll of honour at the entrance to the offices. Shortage of labour had handicapped them very much, and they had been compelled, like other firms; to employ rfemale labour wherever possible. . Leading Features of the Accounts..

Referring to the balance sheet, large deprecia- tions had been allowed for, especially in the pattern values. In the profit and loss account the balance (after charging depreciation) from trading ac- count, interest on investments, cottage rents, and transfer fees, amounted to £22,213 lls. 11d.; deduct the debenture interest paid and what was due to the end of the year, amounting to £2,816 10s., also the directors' and debenture trustees' fees. £509 18s. 5d., and the balance became £18,887 3s. 6d. Add ,to this balance £5,485 is. 84., the amount brought forward from last year, and they arrive at a total of £24,372 Bs. 2d. Deduct from this £1,500, the interim dividend paid on the preference shares at six per cent. per annum for the half-year ended ,T.lne 30th, 1915, and there remained a balance of 4:4?2.872 Bs. 2d.

With that sum the directors now recom- mended a final dividend on the preference shares the six months ended December 31st, 1915, ,rhich would absorb £1.500; a dividend of five iy.r cent. on the ordinary shares for the year, -rhich would absorb £5,000; a bonus of five per r—rt. for the year on the ordinar; shares, ab- . _

£5000; that £5,443 11s. 9d. be trans- ..',-,rred to the reserve fund; and that the balanee •-•+' £5.928 16k. sd. be carried forward to next year's account. -

Paying off Debentures.

Cash in hand and at bankers had decreased considerably, in consequence of tnerr paying off a large number of the old debentures. Invest- ments were less this year,owing to the temporary loan to the Warrington Corporation having been called in to assist in paying off these debentures. and to the cancellation of 47 debenture bonds whieh the company held. The directors had written down the value of the freehold land in front of the works by the amount saved in the redemption of the old debentures, less the cost of the issue of the new ones. There were now only 590 debentures of MO each; he would be glad to know that the debenture issue had been reduced by the sum Of £37,500. (rlenr, hear.) The company's trade with the Colonies had been much hampered owing to the war, and there had been a decrease in the Colonial busi- ness in conseeuence. Continental trade had fallen off considerably, and what business they were doing with neutral countries was most diffi- cult to deal with owing to the crisis. (Hear, hear.)

PRESENT ASSURANCE AND FUTURE

GUARANTEE.

Mr. Clanahan, seconding, said the report would meet with the commendation 'of all the shareholders. In the existing conditions the accounts were highly satisfactory. A point which was particularly gratifying to him was the steady diminution of the debenture loan and the steady increase of the reserve fund. In those two items they had the assurance of their present prosperity and a guarantee of their future advance.

The resolution was passed, and the payments of dividends and bonus, as well as the transfers to reserve fund and next year's accounts out- lined in the chairman's statement, were a'dopted. Messrs. W. Fairhurst and J. H. Kemp were re-elected directors: and Messrs. Edwin Gintherie and Co., Manchester, were re-elected auditors. A hearty vote of thanks to the Chairman concluded the proceedings.

DISPUTE SETTLED?

Education Committee & Health Committee Coming To Terms.

Councillor Henry Roberts, • presiding at the annual meeting of the Education Committee on Monday evening, made the gratifying announce- ment that the dispute between the Medical In- spection Sub-committee of the Education 'Com- mittee and the Health Committee of the Town Council is within measurable distance of being satisfactorily settled—so near, in fact, that the members who recently resigned from the Medi- cal Inspection Sub-committee allowed them- selves to be re-elected. Councillor Roberts proposed that the follow• - ing form the Medical Inspection Sub-committee for the ensuing year :—Alderman James Evans, Councillors Archer, Pox, Jenkins, and Joseph, Rev. G. C. Briggs and Mr. Edwin Plinston, with the addition of Mr. Kingdon, Mr. Parker, Mr. Upson and Mrs. Jackson Holmes. The long- standing difference between the Health Com- mittee and themselves was, he said, rapidly approaching a settlement, He was not in a position to say what was the nature of the correspondence that had passed between the 'Board of Education and the Corporation, but ' he thought he might say this much: that the principle for which the Medical Inspection Sub- committee had contended had been largely con- ceded, and that the suggestion that had been made to the Board of Education by the Health ' Committee was one that would meet with their approval as being fair and reasonable. In these circumstances be thought their old members might rejoin and assist, shoulder to shoulder, the other authorities in the town that were working for the welfare of the children. Alderman Bennett seconded. The informa- tion was exeeedingly welcome, for the loss of these members to the committee was nothing less than a public calamity. - The resolution was carried.

Independent Methodists and War Service.

A letter from the War Office, published in the "Blanchester Guardian" this week, states that the War Department of the State recog- nises the status of Independent ministers, and gives them exemption from service under the Military Service Act. The ministers of this denomination, which is well represented in Warrington and district, perform the services of the Church gratuitously. Mho fact that they are not salaried has not weighed with the authorities, who recognise them as possessing the full status of ministers.

Death of Mrs. Corrin.

Mrs. Jane Corrin. 11e 2‘z mlbgnn-street, Manchester-road, died, aged seventy, on March 3rd, at the home of her son, Mr. John H. Corrin, Wichita, Kansas, U.S.A. Mrs. Corrin, who had a short illness, and was buried at Maple Grove Cemetery, March 6, was a well-known attendant at the Legh-street Primitive Metho- dist Chapel up to six or seven years ago. Her son, at whose house she died, was formerly foreman for Messrs. Hesketh, decorators. He was a tenor soloist, and was very well known in musical circles, being one of the originators of the Male Voice Choir.

Death of Mrs. W. IV. Yates.

As will be teen from a notice in our obituary column, Mrs. Yates, of Elmwood-terrace, Dews- bury, died on Tuesday. Mrs. Yates was born in Warrington in April, .1831, and would have been 85 years of age next month. She was married at the Warrington Parish Church to Mr. W. W. Yates, the well-known Yorkshire journo:-, for a short period in early life was engaged on a local paper now defunct. The marriage took place in March, 1852 so that the diamond wedding of the worthy Old couple was celebrated four years ago. Mr. Yates will command the sympathy of a largo circle of friends, private and professional.

Disastrous Fire.

The Warrington Fire Brigade were engaged seven hours yesterday in subduing an outbreak. of fire at the Mersey 'Fish Meal and Glue Works, situated on the river bank between Statham and Martinscroft. When the brigade arrived under Superintendent Turner, with the steam lira engine, they found the boiler sheds and milling room blazing furiously. A good supply of water being obtainable from the river several powerful jets were. soon got to work. The flames had secured a firm hold, and despite the efforts of the brigade the buildings and their contents were destroyed. The adjoining buildings and store- rooms were in danger, but after several hours' hard work the firemen were successful in con- fining the area of the fire to the buildings in which it originated. The origin is unknown, but the damage is covered by insurance.

A Simplified Bible. A most useful book, arranged and edited by Canon Morley Stevenson, Principal of the War- rington Training College, and C. W. Bailey, Headmaster of the Holt "Secondary School, Liverpool, has just appeared. It is called "The Old Testament for Schools," but it will not only be of value there. The work will satisfy a ,distinct want felt by all parents and guardians who teach the Old Testament to children.

The present re-editing of the text is to avoid the portions unsuitable for the child's mind, and to give, without repetition, a connetted account This is done in a most skill9l manner, in the words of the Bible itself. The narrative is given under appropriate headings, but not as chapter and verse, and the volume contains a very sug- gestive and useful chronological table. The passages from the Prophetical Books are inlserted in their correct chronological order in the Historical Books. This is a new and most valuable feature, and makes the book a chrono-

logical whole. The selection and arrangement are based upon .the wide and-varied experience of two practical teacher.s, and the mime of the Principal of War- rington Training College is sufficient guarantee of the value of the book, which contains over five hundred pages, and is published at 2s. 6d. by Women. Liberal War Workers.

The sewing class conducted at the John Cros- field Memorial Hall every Monday by the War- rington Women's Liberal Association, continues to send out comforts of various kinds to the men under arms at home and abroad, directly and through the various officially recognised authorities. Thdividual parcels are being sent as usual; and comforts in bulk have been sent to the 4th South Lancashire Equipment Com- mittee; socks, scarves and mittens to the 2/4th West Lancashire R.F.A. per Mr. P. C. Garnett; socks, scarves and mittens to the Warrington Voluntary Organisation; and socks to the High- gate V.A.D. Hospital, of which Lady Crosfield is the Commandant. Towels have been sent to Raddon Court Military Hospital and Thelwall Heys Convalescent Home. Generous support has been forthcoming in the way of funds for purchasing material. Special attention is drawn to the work of Mrs. Collin, Legit:street, and Mrs. Pereival, Grafton- street, members of the committee who, assisted by their husbands, have organised the Satur- day eveiiing whist drives, -which, so far, have produced a clear contribution to the funds of £3O. TABLET OF HONOUR.

Scholarship Winners Eulogised By Oki School.

What struck one particularly at the unveil- ing of a largo tablet at the Arpley-street Schools on Tuesday afternoon, in honour of the seventy boys, who, since 1882, have gained scholarehips at the Warrington Grammar School was the pleasing preciseness of every- thing. As the Chairman of the Education Commit- tee (Councillor Henry Roberts) and the man- agers of the school entered the large room, all the scholars immediately stood up and deferen- tially exclaimed: "Good afternoon, sirs." The tablet had been veiled with green cloth, and on Councillor Roberts pulling the cords, the cur- tain fell apart with celerity and' revealed a large mahogany tablet,- with the names' of the distinguished scholars emblazoned in letters of gold. The scholars showed their well-trained musi- cal ability in the singing of "Land of Hope and Glory," "Fairy Elves," and "I come with my love," and, at the conclusion, the National Anthem, with Air. W. 11. Payton at the organ. The Fortunate Many. The names on the tablet are as follows:- 1882.—E. J. Radford, F. W. Wood, W., Cook. 1883.—W. A. Stone.

A. Owen, C. Burdett, William Throssell, L. J. Mulha,ll. Mr. J. Rymer Young presided, and the visi- tors included the Rev. Horace Gray (head- master of the Grammar School), Alderman Tinnion, Alderman Bennett, Mr. R. H. Mel- drum, Mr. J. Longland, Mr. C. Jackson Holmes, and Mr. J. M. liurray (director of education). An Old Boy's Stirring Message.

Councillor Roberts, having unveiled the memorial, said he had the honour to be one of the oldest of the old People's College boys, for, as a lad of five, he entered as a scholar the old Newton-street School, and there found• Mr. Wallington, who had been to him a conscien- tious and consistent friend ever since. He was proud of the school, not simply because he belonged to it, but because as chairman of the Education Committee he knew what a high place it held in the educational system of War- rington, and how highly it was thought of by the Board of Education. He hoped that before many years were over, in addition to the names of those who had won Grammar School schotar- ships, there •would he the names of 'those who had won through to the Universities, via the Secondary School, as well -as the Grammar School. In another part of that room there was a list of the boys who had gone from that school, and grown into manhood, and, on the field of battle, made the greatest sacrifice possible for the country to which they belonged. The spirit which had actuated these boys had to a great extent been fostered—if not 'created—and strengthened by the character of the education they had received in that school. 'He hoped the lessons exemplified by their self-sacrifice would be so borne in upon them that they would be determined to carry them out in their daily " life, and endeavour to make the world nobler than they found' it. (Applause.) The Rev. Horace Gray, of the Grammer School added his congratulations. Mr. Wallington remarked that there had been nothing like that event in the history of the school before. s The tablet contained seventy names, and his predecessor, Mr. Twiss, Who took a great interest in the school examina- tion, was responsible for the success of the first twenty-four • boys. Ile (Mr. Wallington) also expressed his indebtedness to Mr. Isaac Bowen, who for many years had devoted a con- siderable amount of time in preparing the boys for the examination. They were delighted to know that the boys who had gone through that school to the Cframaroar School ,had even such a good account of theunetree:. (Applause.)

FRANCE AND AMERICA.

Mr. Seddon Speaks Optimis- tically After His Visits.

OUR ALLY'S RESOLVE.

The weather has a lot to answer, ler

• _ The blizzard in the Midlands and the South of 'rtgland having disorganised the railway traffic, large audience in the pad' Hall on Thursday Ativit' was almost robbed of a ralnable speech from Mr. J A. Seddon.

The meeting bad the blessing of all political Parties, and was timed to commence at eight o'clock, but it ,Was tI2 ,before Si.don arrived in Warripgto.o on the 4415 train*. Consequently, his 'speech had tis be consid,er- ,a.bly curtailed.

IN THE U.S.A.

• He began by remarking that in divers direc- tions there. seemed to be a feeling of tiredness arising out of the present terrific struggle, and it was just that feeling which was giving an opportunity to the only people who could destroy our cause—the people who were want- ing to make peace. But there could be no lmaco until Germany and her allies were'abso- lately crushed and beaten. He himself be- lieved that war was hell, but there were some things even worse than war, and that was national disgrace and individual dishonour. "It has been my privilege to pay a visit to the I 'United States since the war began," went on Mr. Seddon. "I met all °lasses of people from the President downwards. and what struck me more forcibly than anything was that outside the German born or imported. every one, no matter from what part of the world he or she had come„ has a great adrairakion for Great Britain, declaring that she has not only been loyal to her Allies, but self-sacrificing to a degree, even risking her Impire itself: One of her representative politicians said to me 'Your country has been a great educator in the world. Sho has given the people free institutions, and her experience has been it guide in the Ameri- can Ctustitution, but I venture to say this, that when the historian comes to record this world-wide struggle, his verdict will be that (4mA Britain never did anything more in ac- cordance with righteousness than when she risked her Empire's existence for her bonded word to a small nationality.' " • (Loud ap- plause.) Neutralising German Propaganda.

Mr. Seddon touched upon his visit to France, remarking that along with other Englishmen,' it had been his privilege to try to neutralise the German propaganda which sought to con- vince the French people 'that the English were not doing their share. Tile memory of 'the eight meetings which the had addressed in France would always remain .with him. When be told them of how the workers had risen as one man, that we had raised an army of three million men, and that our Navy was guarding our ,Shores and their's, their faces began to shine and the answer was terrific. The answer was given to him by an old Frenchman, *ho said, "Yob. don't knoW what it means to us here to have England on our side. If Eng- land deserts us that terrible toll in, human life in France Will have been for nought, and the enemy upon our soil will continue their most brutai cuutiuct, but we know that with you behind us nothing but victory can come to\the killed cause." for a long time the people worked twelve hours a day for seven days a week, and owing to their health giving way the Alovernment had de- clared that the workers, or especially the women,, must not work more than eix days per week. This manager told him that his greatest difficulty was to.compel these women to take one day's rest in seven—(applause)—for every one , was either a sister, mother, .or sweetheart of ' some by in the trenches, or .in preparation for the tie6e'n4 tic te.J- that one moment lost might jeopardise the life of their kin. As the days went by and the obituary notices got larger it would behove. to set our hearts against any 'craven desire for peace before victory has come, concluded Mr. Seddon. This war leave the world changed, institutions were in the melting-pot, classes would understand each other better than be- fore—for Men could not live in the presence of death .Month after month and then come back with hatred in their souls—and if they were to be without the fear of another great war in the 'Mitre,. this one must be the final death- blow' to Germany.. CAPTAIN ISAAC SANKEY.

Wounded In The Head During Bombardment.

Captain Isaac Sankey, of the South Lancs. Regiment, son.of Mr. Jacob Sankey, of Church- street, has received a bullet wound in the lead, and now lies in hospital. There•was a bombard- ment at the time.

He is also Captain and Adjutant of the St. Elphin's Company of the Church Lads' Bri- gade.

PRIVATE J. FAULKNER.

Resume,' Duty After Recovering From •Injury.

Private Jack Faulkner, who before the war lived with his "sister-in-law, at 3, Kean-street, wt S recently slightly wounded with shrapnel just 'above his eye, but has now sufficiently re- covered to go back to duty. Private Faulkner, who has neither father nor mother, is 21 years of age. He enlisted three years ago, end before being called up for ser- vice at the outbreak of the present war, worked at Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons, Ltd. Another brother, who is also in the Army, now lies in hospital, suffering from appendicitis.

WAR OFFICE AND CASUALTY LISTS.

The Press Bureau announce that the Govern- ment has decided that in future lists of casual- ties should give no particulars either of the theatre of war in which the casualty occurred or of the battalion to which the officer or man belonged. This decision,has been arrived at in the pub- lic interest, and is a matter of military neces- sity.

It is also requested that the particulars above referred to may not be xneLtioned or published in cbitnary notices sent to the, Press by rela- tives or friends.

We shall be glad if our readers will send us particulars of casualties. Only particulars that comply with regulations will be published.

THE WAR BUDGET.

"Zeppelins in Sea Fights" is the subject of an, illuminating article by Mr. F. A. Talbot, in this week's issue of the "War Budget." The writer, lays bare many of the German plans, and reviews their strategy in such naval engagements as they have had the misfortune to take part in. It appears from this article that large airships of the naval type may be expected to play an important role in the great sea fight, if and when Tirpitz's successor dares to face the music) of our 15-inch guns. Edmund Dane; 'in his' "Story of the Great War," discusses the new outlook after Verdun, especially in relation to Russia's preparation for a great offensive, *ben the signal is given to deal the final 'blow. The latest German loan oames in for an analysis, which leaves little in hard cash to show for the incomparable sacrifice of men on the banks of the Meuse. MESSAGE FROM THE KING., A UNITED FREE CHURCH.

Sir Frederick Milner Visits Lord Derby Hospital.

SPEECH TO BRAVE MEN.

On Tuesday, Sir Frederick Milner, represent- ing Their Majesties,, the King and Queen, visited the Lord Derby Hospital, Winwick. Sir Frederick was introduced by the 'Acting Administrator, Major Rodgers, who informed , the men that as the King was unable to attend personally' he had instructed Sir Frederick to I visit' the hospital on his behalf. Sir Frederick said it was a- great privilege for him to be there and to be able to convey to them the hearty message of sympathy from Their Majesties, the King and Queen, but before doing so be would give them .a short account of what the society to which he be- longed was doing in the interests of the men. He said he was too old to take any active part in the campaign, but he had been trying to help by giving his time to the society, which was called the Incorporated Soldiers' and. Sailors' Help. Sir Frederick explained the various ramifications of the society, and the provision made for helping crippled fighters who might have lost an arm or leg in the country's service—how workshops were being built in various parts of the country so that work could be found for the men and their wages would supplement the- pensions they were receiving from the State. Sir Frederick remarked that by instituting toy-making they hoped eventually to take away the toy trade from their German enemies. Sir Frederick concluded his address by giv- ing the men the stirring message of sympathy from Their Majesties, who informed the men how deeply they sympathised with them in all their sufferings and hardships, that they realised how nobly they had borne them, that they prayed they would soon be restored to health, and also wished to say how proud they were of their brave men, who, by the good conduct they had shown, had won the esteem and admiration of the eivilised world.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
GROCERS AND SUGAR. A Reply To Mr. Harold Smith,

MP.

To the Editor of " The Examiner." )

Sir,—May I be permitted, through the mgdium of your widely-circulated paper, to reply to Mr. H. Smith, member for Warrington, to the ques- tion put by him to the Chancellor of the Ex- chequer in the House of Commons on Thursday week. Mr. Smith's question casts a reflection upon the grocers of the country which the true facts of the case cla not warrant. If the honourable member had only written to one of the grOcer constituents of his own borough it would have saved him from appear - as a lob-sided business man. His question that retail grocers were compelling their customers to buy tea in. order to secure sugar has so alarmed him that he appeals to the powers that be. Now, Mr. Editor, apart from .the Chancellor's reply, which I fear was not satisfactory to the honourable member, the man in the street knows that there has been a shortage of supply. Grocers have had to discriminate,between their customers who are buying their usual weekly supply, and others more favourably armed with reserve capital, who are laying in stocks regard- less of the weaker members of society who can- not afford to speculate with their small weekly incomes. The up-to-date grocers' standard of supply- ing sugar to customers, viz., giving them a quantity equivalent to their order for other household goods, is surely a reasonable one. At the commencement of the war a rush was made upon grocers' stock. Motor-cars were used by excited well-to-do people; as much as four hundredweights viAs seen taken away by a single nouseholder: Results, stocks were depleted, prices advanced to the small weekly 'purchaser which should. have been borne by an entire patriotic community. Yet another reason why customers do not get all sugar they order from the grocer is because they order tea and butter or margarine from tea and butter shops, the profits upon which are. double that which the grocers have upon sugar. The fact that tea shops refuse to handle sugar is proof of this. Why should the grocer do the drudging work, and supply sugar at a minimum of profit to tea-shop customers, when the proprietors of these shops are securing a maximum of profit, quite double that of the

grocers to-day. _ Let the public give an all-round order to the grocers and judge of the quality and price for themselves, even in these war times they will find their weekly orders for sugar not refused them. Let the member for Warrington try the experiment 1,33- my suggested plan of ordering his groceries and he will find his grievance re- moved, and his opinion changed in regard to the grocers of the country. Thanking you in anticipation of publication.—Yours, Ste. JOHN MAW. 1, 53, Prenton Park-road, Prenton, Birkenhead.

MR. SMITH'S QUESTIONS.

The following are the questions, to which exception is taken in the above letter, and the replies for the Gpvernxnent:— Mr. 11. Smith (II—Warrington) asked the President of the Board of Trade if he was aware that in many cases wholesale grocers, as a con- dition to the sale of 'sugar, were stipulating that the purchasing retailer should also purchase tea, often of a specific brand,, and ,that this stipulation was consequently imposed by the retailer on the public. Mr. McKenna (Chancellor of the Exchequer) replied that any such cases should be reported to the Secretary of the Royal Commission on' the Sugar Supply, when they would be investi- gated. The practice, however, of selling sugar to purchasers of other goods, unless abused, might act as a tiafeguard against undue pur- 4hases by those of the public who desired to accumulate stocks in order that they should not have to share in any economy that the national interest might require. Mr. Smith asked whether the practice was not operating hardly on Spoor people, who were compelled to buy a brand of tea they did not desire and in excess of their requirements. Mr. McKenna: I rather gather that the question dealt with wholesale grocers. ("No.") Of course, cases of abuse will be investigated. _ _ _

, Mr. H. Smith .remarked that the practice was a growing practice, and the injustice suffered , was very great. (Hear, hear.) Did not the ' right hon. gentleman consider that the obliga- tion to purchase other articles might be used as a means of indirectly increasing prices? Mr. McKenna replied that he was aware of that. lie hoped the shortage of sugar would be only temporary. It was due to the shortage of ;ships. He 'thought that on the present aCale it .would be over almost immediately, and he hoped .the -trouble would then disappear. Answering Mr. W. Thorne (Lab.—West Ham), who said the Government had promised that sugar should not exceed 4d. a pound, Mr. McKenna, said he understood the general price was 44d. a pound. Mr. Thorne: You go andbuy a pound of sugar and you will .see what they charge you. (Laughter.) . In reply to Mr. A. M. Scott (L—Glasgow), Mr. McKenna said 'brewers' sugars have not been included in the scope of the operations of the Royal Commission.

STAGE AND KINEMA.

Discussed By The Federation At Royal Court. Warrington. • Mr. Harry Foxwell, the producer of "The Vicar's Wife,' being presented here this week, tells me that ever since it was first staged three THE SOULS OF NATIONS • months ago in Manchester, it had attracted good audiences.

The decay of the denominational ideal amongst Free Churchmen and the growing necessity foe a United Free Chuech movement in this country was the chief -topic under discussion at the annual meeting of the South-West Lanca- shire Federation of Free Church Councils, held in the Wycliffe Memorial Buildings on 'Thurs- day afternoon and evening. There was a good attendance of delegates froln the towns and vil- lages included in the South-West Lancashire area. _

In the afternoon a sermon was preached by the Rev..T. Nightingale, of Southport, followed by a tea-table -conference, over which Mr: C. E. Parker presided. Au address was deliverei by the, Rev. A. Harvey (secretary of the South- West Lancashire Federation) in support.of the general idea of the United Free Church of Eng- land. Other speakers included the Rev. T. R. Dann. secretary of , the Liverpool Federation; Rev. T. Nightingale, and Alderman Bennett. The Chairman expressed his sympathy with the ideals of the scheme, as, in his opinion, it was necessary to prevent the wasteful -over- lapping in the work of the Free Churches which took place in various parts of the country. Federalism would unite the different sections o‘ Nonconformity.

• The leading actress Miss Aimee Grattan Clyndes, has been to *Warrington several times, usually with her father, Mr. J. 11. Clyndes, the well-known impersonator of Napoleon. On the last occasion 'ale led In "The Man From Utah." "You know it," she said to me. "I was the most wicked of the dozen wives. I'm supposed to be very good in those 'snaky' parts, but I prefer the antithesis. I have been greatly complimente3 on my presentation of Celia Stanmere. Here I have a chance to dis- play all the emotions—pathetic,* farcical, &v."

Miss Clyndes has had a tconsiderable ex- perience on the music hall, and when Miss Wishe Wynne settled down in London for "The Street of Adventure" furore, she played some of her parts on tour for her.

• Very fittingly Mr. G. Raymond Wallace scores a success as John Stanmere, the yeoman farmer. He happens to be the son of an Essex farmer. He is probably one of our youngest actors in heavy parts, being only 26 years old. One would not judge so when looking at him from the auditorium. He has been on the stage since he was 18 years of age, before which he did not take part in village theatricals.

He always plays the "lead:' in Mr. Fox- well's productions, including that in "The Broken Heart," "Pride of the Mill" and "Mother."

The visit of Ernest E. Norris and Co. in repertoire next week should prove a strong at- traction, for they are to produce three capital plays. "The Lion and the Mouse" is by Charles Klein, the gifted author of "Potash and Perlmutter." On Thursday "John Glayde's Honour," by Alfrecl Sutro, is to be staged, and on Friday "The Walls of Jericho," by the same author. All these plays have enviable qualities of wit and drama.

Hippodrome. Naturally, the first act of Little Mary, on my being introduced, was the putting out of her hand and losing it in mine. It was almost as small as a baby's, and quite as delicate.

While being extremely diminutive—her height is 41 inches and she weighs practically only as many pounds, though 22 years of age— she has the advantage of having a 's7mmetri- cal and beautiful appearance, which is far from the case with some Liliputians. She takes part in conversations quite vivacioukly, too. There is no saying to her "Little girls should be seen and not heard." She has too bright a wit.

Little Mary was, born in Finland. She told me her name, and when I floundered with the spelling she took one of her professional visit- ing cards and wrote it on the obverse side. It was: Mary Hermanovitch.

"So you are married?" I postulated on observing that she was wearing a wedding ring. " Oh, yes !" she replied winsomely. "My manager is my husband. We got married three years ago." Her managtr is the good-sized Russian seen patrolling the stage as she walks on the ball and dances on the tight-wire.

Those amazing acrobats, the Five Wartons, are certainly the cleverest in their branch. They are Belgians and, curiously enough (but not deliberately) anticipated the arrival of the Germans on their native soil by coining to Eng- land a week before war broke out.

"Step This Way," which is coming next week, was the first revue staged, and has been a winner all along. In its appeal it does not rely on gorgeous scenery. There is no over- crowding of the stage with that kind of para- phernalia, which sometimes obscures artiste' weaknesses.

Each lady and gentleman who appears is Al. I have seen them elsewhere in the same show.

Star Klnema. The Suvla Bay film created a great deal of interest, and perhaps more soldiers visited the house while it was on than they have done hitherto in the same period.' I may mention that there was a special performance for the Grammar School boys on Tuesday morning.

"The House of a Thousand Candles" is now being greatly enjoyed. It closely follows the novel from which it has been adapted.

Next week's programme in the first part in- cludes a fine London exclusive film entitled " Charity Ann," featuring Elizabeth lisdon, the charming English actress.

For the end of the week there is a romantic drama entitled "Queen Mother," adapted from the play which was staged at the Royal Court Theatre a few months ago before crowded houses.

Amongst the forthcoming attractions is 'When We Were Twenty-one," founded on the famous comedy, which the Warrington Amateur Dramatic Society produced some time ago, with Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., in a leading role.

FOYER.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
DENOMINATIONALISM WEAKER.

The Rev. A. Harvey said it was at Hull six years ago that the Rev. J. H. Shakespeare, in a brilliant and memorable paper on "The Free Churches and the National Life," first intro- duced the subject of the United Free Church of England to the notice of the National Council of the Evangelical Free Churches. Everyone thought, or at least hoped, that scmething of a practical nature would ensue from the delibera- tions; but six year had gone by, and it had waited until Mr. Shakespeare, on being called to the presidential chair of the National Council, should bring the question forward in a definite form. The years that had intervened had indeed been lean years for Christian work, years in which there had been an almost con- tinuous decline in church members or congre- gations, in Sunday school teachers and scholars. It was obvious to all that things were not as well‘with them as they would desire them to be. Whether for good or ill the denominational , spirit was weaker to-day than it was twenty or even six years ago, with both ministry and

laity; each passed from one denonaination tt,

another as though altogether unconscious of arty dividing barrier. Old feuds had died out, and the things which divided them now were little more than those of forms of government or an ordinance. Our divisions did not appeal either to the conscience of our own people or to the conscience and intellect of the nation. They could never make their full impact upon the nations felt whilst they were split up into sections. Their influence was out of proportion to their numbers. How easy would it be to deal with the disgraceful and wasteful cases of I overlapping. Such ideals could not be reached without labour and sacrifice; but if the end was worth attaining no consideration of difficulty ror sacrifice should deter them. The difficnjties were not insuperable if the new ideal was in ' harmony with the mind of God.. For even a .United Free Church would be no good if it was not the Church of God.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0134.txt | included=true | included =====
A DUAL IDEAL.

'Alderman 'Bennett said he had all his life had two ideals in front of him: (1) the federa- tion of the British Empire; (2) the federation of the Christian Churches. He believed that the federation of the Free Churches would inevit- ably lead to the federation of all Christiai; Churches of the world. It must be the ultimate outcome if their religion was true and if they believed in their religious principles, and he thought that Was the goal to which they should strive. It did not mean uniformity, but it did mean that on, all vital matters which were for the good of the world they, would be unani- mous. On those :lines they could go forward with courage and hope, and live to see that good time coming which good men had struggled for through the past ages. A resolution was unanimously passed approv- ing of* the general idea of the United Free Church of England, and urging the affiliated councils to discuss the proposal and to further it by every reasonable means.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0135.txt | included=true | included =====
NO BELIEF IN ".ISMS."

A public meeting was held in the evening in the Memorial Hall. The Rev. W. T. Derry pre- sided, in the absence of Mr. C. E. Parker. The Chairman said he had come to the con- elusion that where the Free Church Council din some definite work the best elements in thou' church life were united. Where it existed in a vague kind of way he . believed the Free Church Council was an ignominious failure. In the opinion of the Rev. T. R. Dann, of Liverpool, there was no justification for thy existence of denominationalism. In pursuit of their ideal to-day denominationalisni had gon€ for ever, and. its own teachers did not believe in it. Now that their separation had spent and exhausted itself they needed to draw to- gether in a compact force to hit the devil and hit him hard. He believed that the reason why the working classes had forsaken thc churches for the unattached missions was be- cause they did not believe in a religion* spoken with the tongue in the cheek. They did not believe in " isms " which took the place of Christ.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0136.txt | included=true | included =====
SUPREMACY OF THE MORAL FACTOR.

"It is a great _thing to be possessed with an I ideal, but it was a greater thing to be possessed I by it,". declared the Rev- T. Nightingale in .11 powerful address.. The war was changing many things, and, he believed it was having a perma- nent emphasis on. spiritual values. It .was 'shaking things, and the things that could not be shaken would 'remain. If the nations of Europe did not find their sours after the war they had no souls to find. lie was optimistic enough to believe that all was progress. The value of life was in its faith and in its affirmation. The two great foundations of a nation were industry and morality, and drink was the opponent of both and agitinst good citizenship. The king- dom- of God stood for the supremacy of the moral factor, and .the next factor .to God was that of man. The development of the .Free Churches had ,been one Of the most remarkable things of the centuries:. Side by side with that marvellous development there had grown up anomalies: There had been a waste of energy, money, and opportunity, and it was time they were put away.. The; pedestrian church was no use in a motor age. Multiplication of .units was never in the mind of Jesus: it was that they all might be one. Federalism was not the sus- pension of denominationalism, but was a method for its greater efficiency; Their churches were Withont- stride, and. he believed Federalism would give them a high majestic moment.

IMMEDIATE DUTY OF THE CHURCH.

'rho Rev.. 'l'. T. Jamc4 said the scheme 6f

federation was so important that they who were perhaps not able to speak with certainty- about the details were willing that the matter should. be considered by the denominational authorities, as it certainly deserved. ,They must not run away With the idea that any mere rearrange- ment °Mile. Free Churches of England could give them that direct -result in their efforts for the

of. God .if they had not that spiritual reality and enthusiasm which was so necessary. There were several things which he considered we're theimmediate duty of the Church, and one of the firs-t was not to let our lads who had gone forth to battle think that they were forgotten by the churches to which they were attached at home. There never was such a field of enter- prise for the Church of Jesus Christ as there was ' to-day. They had not sent three or four million Church members to the Front. They had sent ' the worst as well as the best. Some lads would come back saddened by the fact that they had succumbed to temptation. Ile had seen some spiritual tragedies—as well as physical ones. Some had come back a lot further from the kingdom of God than when they went. It was the business of-the Church to look to them. When the lads came' back they would not be worrying about what they called a Church, Or about its name: they would be 'asking whether

_ •it was doillg Christ's work—whether it-was play- ing a part in making this land a better land and bringing it to a. sweeter and purer condition. ; :During,. the : •Oveifing. INfi64 ,Ehae7:Hewitt, rendered two solos in a creditable manner.:

LONG MOVES.

Playing Chess With Those At The Front.

Said to be mere pawns, soldiers sometimes play "ducks and drakes" with Kings and Crowns and Bishops. But they must be chess players. Requisitioning the post, several of Warring- ton's best chess players are at present engaged in games with their late secretary, Mr. R. E. Burtt, a Stockton Heath schoolmaster, who is serving abroad in the R.A.M.C. Mr. Burtt enlisted soon after the declaration of war, and, on his resigning the secretaryship of the Chess Club, certain members intimated their I intention of keeping him up to form in his game. 'Moreover, chess moves in a letter are an excellent bonus for interested men. They give one something to think about. Playing of correspondence games always takes ' a long time—unless there is a ".fool's mate." The usual practice is for a player to get an answer to his move by return of post, but Mr. Burtt has to be 'allowed longer than that at _

times. Owing to the. uneerfainty of the post in delivery and the irregular hours Mr. .Burtt Rion? with other .soldiers, •is subject to, .sorne- times al3 ling is ten days elapse before his reply- moves Are received. But, e.s chess players know, tortniSe pace dos not rob the game of its interest. It , T4ever did stumble • through bUstlifig.. those now Act against Mr, Burtt in- clude-Mr. P. Willis, Mr. G. Mason and Mr. R. 'D. Owen. Players At -Winw ick Hospital.

It is an interesting fact that recently Mr. Burtt has met another local chess player, the Rev. W. S. Adams, of Daresbury, who is also in the 'R.A.M.C. Many are the games they have had recently on their pocket boards, which they- carry in their valises wherever they march. . During the session just drawing to a close the club has made good progress in membership. There hits been no stalemate in this respect. Not infrequently some of the members go to the Lord Derby Hospital at Winwick to match themselves with the wounded soldiers, who are fond of the game. &title capital talent has been , discovered, and many enjoyable, though strenuous hours have been spent in hard- thinking in order to obviate 'casualties.

JOTTINGS DV AN OLD JOURNALIST.

A Commendable Exercise.

Even earlier than the far-off period just referred to I took part in a school dramatic reading of Shakespeare's "Julius Cesar," on precisely similar lines to those followed at Stock- ton Heath last wrek. I. can, therefore add my testimony to the usefulness of such form ot entertainment. Of all the imaprtal plays— several of which I have witnessed in the Mean- time--" Julius Caesar" most deeply hitriressed me; indeed, if my experience be any' criterion, last week's effort did more or the " Heathen " participants than they have yet realised. Obvious.

I do not know whether, after all, one ought not to apologise -for thus insisting on what to many readers must really be as" plain as the nose 'on one's face "—like Mr. F.'s aunt's etate- ment made as if announcing a discovery, "There's milestones on the Dover-road." This allusion to the obvious recalls another play, in whtchl Qin stern parent rebukes his demented daughter;- the fair "ritriurina, with— "Thou califkt not sew die Spanish Fleet, Because it is not yet in sight "

which, by-the-bye, is what is very much the matter with the German fleet at present.

Transpontine Drama. I find "Current Topics from Stageland " in- teresting reading, but what mas become of the barnstormers whom " Foyer " doubtless re- members well—the strollers who, having acquired the dignity of a portable booth, cam. and went periodically. To be sure, one of the chief of thesi3 crossed the bar years ago. We youngsters knew him as " Old Wild." I sup- pose that, unlike Barrie's Peter Pan, who never grew up, this ancient worthy of the sock and buskin fraternity was always old; certainly, I do not remember him otherwise, and nobody in my hearing ever spoke of him as ever having been young. Alas, poor Yorick! I knew him well enough to understand that the potted plays —three five-minutes acts and a pantomime to match—which his then pitiably worn-out com- pany were wont to piesent on the fairground in their degenerate days, bore no resemblance at all to the melodramatic powers they dis- played when at the zenith of their histrionic glory they delighted patrons with " Cartouche, 0\47 French Jack Sheppard," "Emmett, the Irish Patriot," " Belphegor, the Mountebank," and (of course) among many others of the lik3 charaoier and of equal antiquity, "Maria Martin, or the Murder in the Red Barn." Poor Old Wild! He fell on evil days, and when at the final performance under his auspices which I happened to attend, the bold had baron ordered his ruffianly retainers to " Ber-ring forth the peerless Ida!" and the fair captive in question proved to be a person who in these spacious times might, and assuredly would have qualified for ,an old-age pension, I realised that " Ichabod " must, with reluctant hands, be in- scribed upon the portals of the doomed Thes- pian temple. Meanwhile, to my certain know- ledge, there have been strolling mummers not without merit: but it must be quite a dozen years ago since any of them came my way. " Quietness."

The address_on this subject at Golborne-street Baptist Church brought to my mind these: "True quietness of heart is got by resisting cur passions, not by obeying them." "Quietness. and peace flourish where reason and justice govern; and true joy reigneth where modesty directeth." Although I have been familiar with these quotations over a couple of generations, I have not traced the 'authors.

"Poor Children's Friend," I am able, however, confidently to affirm that it was Sydney Smith who said:- " If you repre- sent the various parts in life by holes upon a table, we shall generally find that the triangular person has got into the square hole, the oblong into the triangular hole, and the round person has squeezed himself into the square hole." But that has not happened in the case of Conncillor James Skelland, of the Ragged School Mission, whose 80th birthday has just been celebrated. Most emphatically, to use a very hackneyed expression, he has proved him- self the right man in the right place.

Too Old at Forty. It is not so long ago since we were discussing the question "Are men played out at forty?" We no longer speak of this in Gath nor publish it in the streets of Askelon—for, look you, the man of forty is of military sage nowadays. To my point, however, which has been suggested by the still active and vigorous Councillor Skel- land's attainment of the age in question, multi- plied by two. I recall that in 1901 the lamented Mark Twain wrote in the "North American Review" of a visit to John Hay a quarter of ii century before, .he being then 42. Hay, who was two years his junior, was represented as saying in substance this: "At 40 a man reaches the top of the hill of life, and starts down on the sunset 'side. The ordinary man, the aver- age man, it to particularise too closely and say the commonplace man, has at-that age see- ceeded or failed; in either case he has lived all of his life that is likely to be worth recording." And So forth and so on—for, in elaboration cf the argument, there was much more to the lik effect. Mark told how, while not resenting hi 4 share of the verdict, he nursed his "wound." in silence until he was able to show, that the idea that they had already finished their work in life, that neither of them had anything further to do as "benefactors to mankind," was all a mistake. Said he; "I had written four books then, pos- sibly five. I have been drowning the world in literary wisdom" ever since, • volume after volume; since that day's sun went down he has been the historian of Mr. Lincoln, and his book will never perish; he has been ambassador, brilliant orator, competent Secretary of State." "Played out at 40," quotha!

Value of a Ceptury of Life. At a Public Health Congress not so very long ago it was declared that every man has a right to live a hundred years. One of the medical journals comuiented at length upon this, and here is a useful little extract from the article:— " Unquestionably, if a man models his way of

fe on that of -a cabbage, he may vegetate_ a

little longer than if be plays his part on the ' stage of human life. By living wholly for him- self and thinking of nothing but his health, he may keep off death for a little time; but is not such a life a living death?" Of course this contemporary, while by no .means deprecating reasonable care •of . health, urged that to set before people from their earliest days a century of life as the chief thing to be aimed at will do little to help them to attain that ideal, and may do much to make their lives less beautiful and less useful than they otherwise might be. Work,—recreation—rest, reasonably divided, none carried to excess; there lies the secret_ of

health and happiness. There are highly respected old natives of this district who dis- covered that secret long ago, and have used their knowledge to their own and the public advant- age.

MAIMED \CLERK BEFORE THE

MUNITIONS TRIBUNAL.

Having been permanently disabled while in the Army, a soldier returned to his employ- ment with a Warrington firm in December, and, finding that another man held a position over him, appeared before the Munitions Tribunal on Monday, and complained that his employers had unreasonably withheld his certificate of discharge. He now said he had had a better offer and wanted to go to work for a company out of town, who were doing war work con- tinually. He was now earning .22 2s. 6d. per week, and had not had an advance since December, 1913. He had been offered .210 a month by a firm at Birkenhead. He gave his notice a fortnight ago. Mr. T. J. Ridgway (the chairman) asked the representative of the company whether they had refused to grant a certificate to the man, and received a reply that the notice to leave only expired on Saturday, and they had not been asked for a certificate.

Mr. Ridgway: That puts the applicant "out of Court." Certificate refused.

EXEMPTION OF SCHOOL CHILDREN

am obliged to Mr. Knowles for his correction of an obvious mistake I inadvertently made when making some remarks +n the above subject. Speaking (roughly) of the exemptions I should have said of those granted about one. half were in favour of scholars "in," and not ,as having " passed " the VII. and ex-VIl. standards. _ _ _

" Necessity " is most often, but not the sole reason Why exemption is granted. Suitability of proposed employment, physical condition, mental incapacity to profit by the instruction given, &c., are often adequate reasons. The policy of the School Attendance Commit- tee needs no defence. Its members are thoroughly representative; they have personal practical experience in the industrial, business, social, and religious life of the community, give freely of their time to the discharge of a some- what disagreeable public duty, have no selfish interest at stake; they regret their decisions do not give satisfaction to parents and teachers, whose interests in the question of exemption are not identical.

The Working Men's Infirmary and DisPensary committee was created, and exists solely, to assist financially the needs of thistmost deserv- ing institution, where special surgical skill and appliances and medieal aid to relieve pain and suffering is given to those who need its services, many of whom 'would be otherwise unable to obtain it. The committee, through the en- thusiasm and earnest work of its members, has contributed to this object this year a sum of 0,300, and since the creation of the commit- tee the splendid sum of over V 6.4410. R result of which they are justly prend and they would gladly welcome the co-operation of the union Mr. Knowles so ably represents in this very laudable work.

WALTER.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0144.txt | included=true | included =====
DRUNKENNESS AND RESTRIC- TIONS.

Sir,—So far from "running away" from my. position—as Mr. Parkes alleges I do—l wel- come his straightforward question: To what do I attribute the decrease of ' arrests for drunkenness in Control Board Areas? I reply unhesitatingly: Certainly not to the restric- tions! Two very important factors must be taken into account in considering the recent drunkenness statistics.. First the progressive withdrawal of large numbers of the male population, and, secondly, the progressive depletion of the Police Force by its members enlisting. When so many young men are away we may naturally look for less insobriety. and inadequate police arrangements would certainly tend to result in less arrests, even if there were no decrease in actual drunkenness. Again, the restrictions lead to increased home drinking, which generally means that there is considerable secret drunkenness which never figures in the Police Court at all. Rims been openly alleged—by Mr. Horatio Bottomley, for one—that the police were deliberately in- structed to "go easy" on arrests for drunken- ness when the restrictions were in operation. Sir John Rees charged the Government with this in the House of Commons, and no official denial was forthcoming! In London and in Liverpool the arrests for the four weeks pre- cedinK the introduction of the regulations showed a very suspicions leap upwards. It must not be forgotten that Control Board com- parisons of drunkenness figures always take this period as the standard of pre-restriction days. --

Finally, we find decreased arrests for drun- kpnness not only in controlled areas, but throughout the country, which is surely proof positive that whatever this decrease may be due to, it is not due to the Control Board's fantastic ordinances.

J. E. SIMPSON.

" TOMMY " AS POET.

A Celebrated Landing While The "Sea

In Parts Was Red."

Sapper Bert Daniel, of the Royal Engineers, who, befOre the war, was an electrician at the Electricity Works, Warrington, and who is the son of Mr. George Daniel, 13, Forster-street, sends home some verses which have been written by a comrade, to whom, he says, the lines do much credit. Sapper Daniel was one of the last to leave Gallipoli at the evacuation, The lines are as under:—

THE LANDING OF SUVLA BAY...

You talk of Balazlava And of Trafalgar Day: But what of the Eleventh Division Who landed at Suvla Bay?

They were part of Kitchener' Army, Some had left their Children and wivs But they fought for "England's Glory," Yes, fought for the:r very lives.

It was on the Atli of August When they made that terrible dash, And the Turks along the hillside Our boats were trying to smash.

The order came to fix bayonets, As out of the boats they get, Every man that night was a hero Who was .facing the Turkish shot.

Funnels of ships were smashed, - While the ea in parts was red, But they fought their way through the oceau To the beach that was covered with dead.

Creeping at last up the hillside, While shot and shell fell around, They made a last desperate effort And charged over the Turkish ground.

The Turks at last gave it up When they saw our bayonets at play; They turned their backs on the British And retired from Suvla Bay.

There were the Lineolns, Dorsets and Staffords,,` And the Notts and Derbys too, The Border Regiment was also there, The rough and ready crew.

The Yorks, West Yorks and Ridings, The Yorks and Lacs as well, Who fought for good old Yorkshire Were amongst the lads that fell.

Then there were the Xiancheeters, With the L.F.'s by their side, The lads who had come from Lancashire Will fill our 'hearts with pride.

And, far away, on the hillside, Lying beneath the clay, Are some of the lads who gave their urea. At the Landing of 'Soyla Bay.

.

LIVELY TRIBUNAL SCENES.

That Stockton Heath, Grappenhall and Whitley are not without their conscientious objectors lir'a3 evident • at thy yuncorn Rural District Tribunal, at Dutton, on Tuesday, when

• there was more than "a certain liveliness," in 'L fact, at times, the proceedings were boisterous.

CHRISTADELPHIAN NOT A SOLICITOR.

A young Christadelphian, residing at Stock- ton Heath, and employed as a clerk in Warring- ton, applied - for absolute exemption. He was a Christadelphian. The works he was engaged at were under Government control, but he was engaged in a civil capacity. A representative of the Christadelphians asked permission to explain the position of the Society. • .The Clerk said they could not listen to speeches. If he had any evidence the Tribunal would hear it.

.

The representative said he had authority to speak for the young man. The Clerk: You have no authority to make a speech. The Representative: I was allowed to appear at Warrington and to speak there, and yet you won't allow me to • say anything. I .want to show on what our conscientious objections are based. You don't know yet what .. are his conscientious objections. I have a .right to appear. It is in the Act, and I .shalivertainly take the matter up. The Chairman : We conclude you will do this, whatever our decision.

Applicant said lie reckoned Christ was his King, and Him only would he serve. He was prepared to endure and suffer even death for 'His sake, and no man could rob him a his con- victions. . They were based on' the only book that WAS able to make such convictions—the Bible. He was proceeding with u long harangue when

The Chairman asked if the Tribunal was going to sit down Under this sort of thing. They knew exactly what he objected. to and why he objected. Applicant, who was accompanied by several of his fellow members. was asked to retire. .

One of them asked for the military :rer.re- sentative also to leave the room. The Chairman: That is a matter for us. The Member: We have a right to ask for th:. exclusion of the military representative. They left the room, Still protesting, and the military representative later left by another door. •

The application was refused, and applie it stated his intention of carrying his appeal further.

ALREADY DEAD.

A seed traveller, of Higher Whitley, appealed for total exemption owing . to home ties and conscientious grounds. War was nothing but waste to fi Christian's thinking from beginning to end, and all that he could do in the matter -was to pray for peace. At the age of 14 years God brought him into-judgment for wrong- doing and condemned the being then within him, which was the devil, to depart. A terrible struggle took place, which led to the first death. He had to become a new creature. God then breathed Jesus Christ's spirit within him, which was a great comfort, and he had •been under His guidance ever since. If be were in yield to anything pertaining to war he would soon be condemned to a second death, .which would mean everlasting destruction to his soul. His home ties were also becoming heavier daily, his parents being 66 years f age. He had

REFUSED TO PLEAD.

Conscientious Objector's Outburst. A builder's clerk, residing at Granperihall, at present out of employment, applied for absolute exemption, but stated no reason for his appli,:a- tion.

Before stating his case, the appellant asked if there were any labour representatives on the Tribunal, and if so, if he could have their names and occupations, _ _ _

The Clerk: You can get that by inquiring from another source. The Tribunal is here to hear your application, and it is properly consti- tuted.

Appellant: You refuse to answer the ques tion?

Tlio Chairman: We are not here to answer your questions. The Clerk: What are your grounds for exemption? Appellant: I refuse to give any grounds at all. I will not waste your time. ' Appellant's father asked to give the. reason for his son's refusal to give his grounds:When one of the other conscientious objectors was in the room the military -representative called out "Coward." A Member: I heard no such word..

The Father: It was said. A Member: I don't think you heard it either.

The Father: I did not ask you to believe it A Member: Is that the only remark you have to make? )

The Father (to the member): I should not like to trust my honour in your hands.

The Chairman: Will you kindly leave tbe roomP

The Father: 'Ye. I have iust as much r'ght in the room as you have.

The Chairman: You have no. right at all at present. The Fatber: If I had employed your son as a lawyer _I should have been all right..

The Chairman: I have no son who is a lawyer.

Exemption was refused, and the. military representative suggested that .a military .escort should be sent for the appellant that night.

NO OATH* TO THE KING.

A laboratory assistant residing at Stockton Heath, applied on consaentious grounds for absolute exemption from all military service. In his statement of claim ,the appellant men- tioned that he va employed on Government work in the analysis of materials used in the manufacture of soap and glycerine. • The Chairman: Government work means the manufacture of explosives andothat kind of work?—lt is not really Government work. I understood at the time I sent the claim that it was Government work, but it is not Govern- ment work. I cannot take part in any work connected with the Government.

The Clerk: Are you giving up that situation? am.mlt in any way violating my conscience by remaining at .the duties in which I am engaged, because I am not doing any work for the war. • • •

If your employers asked you to take on the analysis of glycerine you would refuee to do BO p—Yes.

A Member: Would you be prepared to take non-combatant service?—None that would re- quire an oath to the King, because I have taken an oath to the King of kings; no matter what deeision the Tribunal come to, I cannot violate my conscience.

Exemption refused and appeal form asked for.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, APRIL 1, 1916.

_ • DISTRICT /I nv3

I,YIVIM

Further Postal Restrictions.—Commencing Monday next, all sub-offices will cease public business at 7 p.m. and the hours for public business at the village office will be between 9 a.m. and 7 p.m.

Thanks to Council Chairman.—Nearly all the members of the Council, at their monthly meeting on Monday, bore testimony to the ability and fairmindedness which Councillor R. IT. Gotobed has shown during the. past 12 months as their chairman.

Met An Old Friend At The Front.—Corporal Henry Brookes,' writing under date March 18th, ti) his Mother; who resides at West Hyde, New- fields, " Lymm, says Last week,, while we were on a long march :we met Mr. Harold fTesketh, in a small village, where the. Royal Flying Corps—with which he .is :serving—were billeted. We just had a chance to ask him how he was, as we passed by. Glad to say I am keeping well, and hope to see you before very long." Corporal Brookes is well-knowh to "Examiner" readers, we having published interesting letters from him from time to time. Congregational Church_ Anniversary Services.

Special services were held at the Congrega- tional Churcli on Sunday in connection with the church anniversary, and were conducted by the Rev. Dr. Scurrah. In the afternoon a sacred concert was given by the Sunday School scholars, under the direction of Mrs. George Willan. On Monday evening the annual- tea meeting was 'held, and 100 people slit down to a hearty repast provided and served by Mes- dames Gill, F. Guest, Brieriey, and Miss Forrest

The Snowstorm: Lymm Soldiers' Experience.- 3apper A. Nicholls visited his home at New-road_ ;n Tuesday night last, on a short furlough from • he front. IntervieWed by our representative, he said " We had a very rough 'passage- while :-!.nssing, and wf 71 we nr“ved Tondon we were welcomed by a great snowstorm, which grew worse later on. • Our journey between London and Leicester was terrible, the train .)illy going at crawling pace, with the result that when we arrived at our 'journey's end we were bout seven hours late." Asked • about the nature of their work, h. said " We do more trench-digging than anything else, and make a lot of gun emplacement. Lpt. Of r,r'- have got to turn our bands to almost anything if need be." In civil life Sapper Nicholis is a miner, and is employed by Levi Thomason, Bank .House. He is 'well known locally, being a chorister at the. Parish Church: Labourer's Sudden Death.—Deputy-Coroner White held an inquiry at the Police Station on Tuesday on the body of William Hill, a labourer, who dropped dead whilst following his employment on Monday morning. -Evidence was given to the effect that the man was en- gaged filling waggons with soil on the Man- chester Ship Canal Embankment at Statham, and had only been employed about two hours when he suddenly collapsed • and died.—Dr. Leonard Thorpe stated that he and his brother —Dr. Harry Thorpe—had made a post-mortem examination and found the deceased to be suf- fering from a slightly enlarged heart and thickening arteries. Asked by the Coroner if he thought the exhaustion caused by the work had anything to do with the man's death, he replied in the negative, stating that the an was liable to die at any moment.—Hill was 59 years of age and lived in lodgings at Man- chester-road, •Woolstom—The jury returned a verdict of "Death from heart failure, due to natural causes." • •

Sergeant .Richard Percival.—Grimy and dis- hevelled and with foreign dust still -upon his boots, 'Sergeant Richard .Percival landed ,at his home, in Newfields, late on 'Monday night. Talking to "The Examinee' Man, .he re- marked: "I left the 'boys' all in good health,' and, I am 'pleased to saY, in good spirits, all eagerly awaiting for the great advance, which will be the beginning of the end.' We are now a pioneer battalion and are engaged on such work as making and repairing roads, &c., and I can-assure you the boys enjoy q, for it is a nice change from the monotonous trench work." Sergeant Percival was mobilised at the outbreak of war, and after training in different parts of the 'country he was eventually drafted out some fifteen months ago. He is .well known in sportity, circles and for several years held the office of hon. treasurer to the local football club. In civil life he follows the occupation of a bricklayer and is employed by Councillor J. H. Holt, of Bars Bank, Statham.

DE JONG-RAPER.

Medical Officer's Son Wed Before Re-Joining Regiment.

Mr. Edward Meyer de Jong, son of the Medical Officer of Health for Lymm, has been married quietly at North Ferriby, East Yorks„ prior to rejoining his regiment. His bride is Miss GladYs Mary Raper, daughter of the late Mr, Charles Raper and Mrs. Raper, formerly of The Grove, Lymm, and now of The Cottage, North Ferriby.

The bridegroom joined the ,Australian Army at the commencement of the war, and was one of the first to land at the Dardanelles. He had three attacks of .dysentery, and so bad was the lasi one that he ;was sent to England. He has now rejoined his • regiment.. • He comes of •a patriotic family, his father being a lieutenant in the R.A.M.C.; his eldest brother, Dr. Oscar de .Jong, is in Malta; and his second brother, Captain Kenneth de Jong, is - with the Sussex Regiment

TRIBUNAL BOMBSHELL.

Threat To Withdraw Custom From Member.

A sensation has been caused in Lymm through the resignation of Mr. Frederick W. Druce, a member of the Recruiting Tribunal. The announcement was made at the monthly meeting of the Lymra Urban Distrint Council on Monday kvening, and as the Clerk (Mr. W. Mullar'd) read Mr. Druce's letter, the Chair- man (Councillor R. H. Gotobed) could not repress an indignant expression of I.'Shamer'

The letter Mr. Bruce -‘,.-rote was as follows : "I wish to tender my resignation as a mem- ber of the Tribunal, as I think it would be better for an independent man and not a man in business in the same place. I have already been told that I shall not get any more trade from a certain place. 1 cannot afford to lose any trade, and I think I am the only man on the Tribunal that it might affect.—Yours faithfully, F. W. DErcE." Mr. Bruce is in business as -harness- maker.

Councilor Ingham: Some- people take a narrow view.

Councillor -Cowper: And le is a .good man, too.

In moving that Councillor Mclntyre sho-1d occupy the position, Councillor Cowper said he did not think the people would be able to frighten him. Councillor Mclntyre: I am afraid Mr. Cow- per cannot be a friend of mine, or else he would not want me to do this. (Laughter.) Councillor Groves seconded, and the pro- posal was carried unanimously, Councillor Mclntyre remarking: "Thanks, but it is a thankless job." At a sitting of the Lymm Tribunal last week Mr. Druce's letter was read.

The Chairman (Mr. 'l'. J. Ridgway) remarked that he waf4 sorry such things were said in the village.

CORPORAL A. E. BURROWS.

Visits Home And Makes A Comment Oji " Conscientious Objectors."

Corporal A. E. Burrows visited his home at Oldfield-road, Statham, on Satur- day, on a short furlough from the Front. His Military career is a very interesting one, for he served 12 years as a Regular soldier, which involved :part of. the South 'African 'Campaign, for which he holds . the Queen's Medal. He afterwards -passed into the Reserve, and was mobilised at the outbreak of war. •

===== 0002601_19160401_art0163.txt | included=true | included =====
Germans Changed Their Tune.

Relating his experiences to our representa- tive, he stated that he commenced his duties during the great retirement from Mons. "At the commencement of the advance," said the gallant Corporal, "we picked up several wounded Germans, who could speak English fairly well and seemed quite confident of vic- tory, and often spoke of the great time they were going to have when Germany had

crashed her enemies; hut prisoners taken lately seem very despondent and often. ask when we think the war will cease."

Asked what he thofight of the conscientious objectors, he said he considered it a disgrace and mighty insult to such a fine branch .of the British Army as his for anyone to suggest putting them in it; but he was afraid that their consciences would very soon alter when they had been out for a short time. - -

"We have received praise from every quar- ter," he said, "and we do not want this good opinion marred by the presence of the scum of the earth."

LYAIM RECRMING AREA.

Official Appreciation Of Work Done.

To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir,—ln compliance with War Office instruc- tions, and with a view to centralising the work, the Lymm Recruiting Area, along with Sale and. li.n.utSford, has now been taken over by R. 0., Altrincham, to whom all correspondence and inquiries should be addressed.. in future. I desire to take this opportunity of tender- ing my sincere thanks to all who have assisted me in any way in the matter of recruiting in this area.. The Officer Commanding the 22nd Recruiting Area has conveyed to me, and Captain Fred J. Gosset, the Chief Recruiting Officer for the county, .hasepersona.ly expressed to me their high appreciation f the admirable and efficient manner in Which the work hak been carried out. This has been brought about by the splendid .spirit of camaraderie that has prevailed throughout amongst all concerned, especially during the strenuous time of the last five months.

As the returns show, in retruiting, as in other ways,,Lymm is proving during the prc- sent crisis to be worthily maintaining its hon- oured traditions of loyalty to the King and country.—Yours, . (Signed) FRED E. EVANS, Late lion. RO., Lymm. March 30th, 1916.

A ss. RISE.

Coke To Help Ke(p Dom). Rates.

Feeling that they were not charging suffi- cient for their coke, the Urban District Council Oh Monday decided to increase it from 13s. 4d. to ,18s. 4d. per ton: _ _

This_step'was initiated by Councillor Parker, who said .that generally :the price of coke was from to ~t 1 2s. 10d. They sold approxi- mately 800- tons of coke per annum, and the 58. increase per ton Would bring in- £2OO, roughly, which meant a 2d. rate. They were up against most trying times, and it was due to them as councillors to make as much profit as they could, in order to keep down the rates. Councillor Critehley seconded, saying that 13s. 4d. Was a., ridiculous price to charge. At the present .-moment the Council could not afford to sq. it at that rate. Councillor_ Cowper said Hilt although his employe& used a lot of coke it was his duty as a councillor to look after the zratepayers. He should assist his hands if they had to nay the advance, but he was of opinion that they ought to charge more. It was their duty to rave r.ll they could. , Proposal adopted as stated.
