LYAIM FINANCES.

Councillor Lockyer On The Rumoured Increase In Rates.

• It having been stated in a newspaper that there was a possibility of the Lymm rates being increased by is. or is. 3d. in the ot, strong repudiation was made by Councillor Lockyer at the monthly meeting of the Urban District Council on Monday evening. Councillor Lockyer said he was afraid there were. a few Germans in Lymm, or it would not have been found necessary for the Chairman of the Council to have written protestieg against such an uncalled for suggestion being made. Before he read the paragraph he thought they were living in a civil. ised countty, but he was afraid tuey had some Germans here. who wanted clearing out. '"it appears, ;according •to the letter, ,that they-want to break Up our 'little town, which we are striving to maintain, and to keep. our heads • above water, he added. "To Pass a • remark that the rates would ,be increased from is. to is. 3d. in the ot is, I think, • ridiculous, because our Clerk (Mr. Mullard) could not give the figures of the rate now if he were asked. We have not yet been through our estimates for the current year, but last year" we made a reduction on the estimates for the roadS of .r.t671 55., and before that a rediiction of .-2513 15s. How, therefore, anyone can put figures in the paper as to the possi- bility of an increase in the rates is, I think, absurd. Why does not the writer of the para..' graph put his name to it? If I put anything in the Press I back it up with my name." Councillor Cowper: I should like to say a word regarding this. I think the paragraph has been put down to me, but that is imma- terial. I have been on the Council two years, and we have never had 'enough money to pay, and the sooner we put is. or is. 3d. on the better. The sooner we put the price uP, pay our debts, and conduct ourselves in iu business- like manner the better for Lyinm and the Connell.

Councillor Ingham: I don't think we can discuss what the probable rate will be.

That ended the incident

Later, however, Councillor Cowper suggested that the Council should call in an "outside, independent auditor," so that they couldi get to know exactly as to where they were. The Chairman replied that the accounts were audited by a Local Government Board gentle- man.

Councillor Coa-per's suggestion fell on stony ground and nothing more was said on the sus: jeet.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0170.txt | included=true | included =====
IDLE LAND.

Hon. J. E. Cross On Blank Spots

In The District.

FARMERS AND WOMEN'S LABOUR.

The Hon- J. E. Cross reckons there is land in the district which should be cultivated in greater measure than it is. He also states that since the Cheshire War Agricultural 'Committee was formed over 500 women have been found places on farms, but the farmers generally are unresponsive when appealed to to employ other hands. than men's.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0171.txt | included=true | included =====
trishmen's Fright and Flight.

He said so at a meeting in Lymm Council Schools„ on Wednesday evening, convened to persuade women to start work on the land and to induce farmers. to be patriotic and wise enough to employ them.

"It is our business to see that there is no land lying waste," he declared. "All has to -be cultivated. .There is some land not far from here—l won't mention names or places—but I will mention that I know in High Legh where I live,, there are one or two small patches shall be very pleased to see cultivated and made the best of. We have got to grow as much:food as we can. You can see the importance of , that, because if the German Navy get past our Navy what a scare there would be and how difficult it would be to get food into: the country. We: should soon begin to feel the pinch." (Hear, hear.) , The speaker observed that there was now a great shortage of /labour on land, especially owing. to the sudden flitting of Irish labourers in September, when they got frightened at the registration scheme. They were in a very serious position with regard to labour and they had got to make the best of it. Farmers who had for the most part simply directed farming would have to do more with their owri hands. Those who had retired or worked leisurely would have to put their hands to the plough again. (Applause.) It was the duty of every- one to give of his or her best. It had been proposed by the Board of Agri- culture to relieve the position by the importa- tion of Danish labour, -wounded Soldiers and women. He (the speaker) himself believed 'in relying on the people of our own country, and if women would come forward that would be poSsible. In Cheshire there were several centres where women were being trained, and now the responsibility fell upon farmers to take and train them completely. He hoped they would rise to the occasion, for there had been a great responSe by the women. Town and Village Women—A Difference.

Miss Knowles, a Lancashire lady, who has been working hafd to organise women for ser- vice ons the land, was convinced that, after training, women ,would be most useful. The women were not coining from the towns, but from the villages. This was what one might expect,, for the town women were not pleased about being without lights and theatres. , Hap- pily, in the 'last few weeks many women had offered themselves as being willing to work on farms, and now it entirely ,rested with the farmers as to whether they had women's labour_

"You will find women all right on the: farms," she added, in a bright passage, "for if they Can manage husbands they can certainly manage the milking. (Laughter.) We are sometimes told that women will not do .the dirty work, but .I know for a fact that those , who have already gone on to the farms do not object to clearing out pig-styes. • They have the: grit to do it, and they don't mind getting , dirty." (Laughter and applause.) Women, however, would have to be treated differently to men. Too much must not be ex- pected at first. Tolerance and patience must be shown, remembering that they were going on to the farms for patriotic reasons and with a desire to help the country. (Applause.) County Councillor Dutton said: it was a far-: mer's bounden duty, in the interests of the nation, to produce .all: he could. lie honestly believed that in times .of war or peace the farmer should not hold up his land, and if he did not make the best use of it, especially at this time, he was nothing less than a traitor to his country. (Applause.) He had no sym- pathy and no patience with the man who was refusing to do his duty and conducting his farm in a happy-go-lucky fashion. (Applause.) Mr. Dutton advised all farmers to :use up- to-date ,methods ;in •their work, utilising sill-, phate of ammonia :as manure. He guaranteed that this would, swell their returns and in that way they, as well as 'the country, would-bene- fit. 11e•ho.ped-farmer's would realise the serious- ness of affairs, because the enemy's submarine policy was hound to effect the food market in a certain measure. If home production were increased the perils of starvation would be , lessened.' (Hear, hear.) If Neutral Ships Fail Us ?

Votes of thanks were passed, on the proposi- tion of Mr. S. W. Gould, C.C., seconded by Mr. Alfred Watkin. The latter mentioned that as a director of a shipping company the war had been particularly brought home to him this week by the sinking of one of their ships. They must remember that not only British ships were employed in bringing food into England, and they might have to face the fact that, by the enemy's submarine policy, vessels from neutral countries might be .de- ployed to other places, where they could obtain equally high rates. (Hear, hear.)

Aged Residents Pass Away.—Two deaths have taken place in the village during the last week. John Davy, of Meadow House, •Whitharrowz road, died on Saturday, after a fairly long illness, at the age of 88; and Charlotte Broadey, of Booth's Hill, passed away on Monday, at the age of 76. Both were •rery well known in the district. They were interred at the, Parish Church on Wednesday and Friday, restively.

REFUSE DISPOSAL. Will. Scavenging Difficulty Force Incorporation ?,

The, Stockton Heath Parish Council are in somewhat. of a dilemma as to how they can dispose. of tiLl dustbin refuse. For many years the 'contractor to .the Council. has had the r.ivilege of using farm land outside the parish for the reception of , the refuse from doio bins and. su!i..ll path.. but the advent of a new estaie agont th.t. hind in question has put a stop ,to the anrisgt.n.ent, as he has an objlctien Lcirg used as a 'tip, for such rubbish.

-The difficulty which confronts the inhabitants was fully discussecLat the annual Parish Meet- ing, held on 'Wednesday night. Already the Parish Council have made an appeal by circu- lars to householders to burn as much ruobisii as possible, so as temporarily to ease the situation. In further support of ;this request a resolution. was passed at the meeting instructing,. the con- traqor ,to refuse to. take away from the dust- bins anything which could be consumed by fire, and that the Council shOuld take steps to obtain a tip inside or outside the parish for such refuse as could not be consumed.. The question has become so serious that it was sug- gested at the meeting that incorporation with the Borough of Warrington was the only. solution.

The question was raised. by Mr. J. Brown, who asKed .whether anything had . been done respecting the conversion of the present pail systent•ta that of the water carriage vs- tem.

Mr. A. Battomley said that the reason why the matter was shelved.. at the present time was because the LOcal. Government Board had appealed to all local bodies to. stay their hand in regard to any new expenditure. If the Council decided to proceed ;further with the scheme the Local Government Board would not give their sanction to. the borrowing of the money at the present time. The Council in a Dilemma.

Mr.. F.. Pearson (chairman of the Sanitary Committee) said it was entirely due to the war that the scheme had been. hung up. The Coun- cil had already decided on a scheme, but they were stopped for the simple reason that they could.-not get, permission to borrow the money. He would like t 9. draw the attention of the parishioners to the serious difficulty the Coun- cil. were experiencing in regard to •their sani- tary matters. For many years they had had the privilege of using Cobb's farm for the reception of the refuse from the dustbins and pails. They had the consent and approval of the late agent of the estate, •Mr. George Warburton, and his successor, Mr. Armstrong, who, he believe,d, was now .with the Forces. A new agent had come upon the scene and the former arrange- ments made with regard to this tip were now. at an end, as the agent objected to the Coun- cil's contractor making use of the land. Warrington Would Help But

In their dilemma they naturally turned to the Warrington Corporation and asked -them if thy could take the refuse. The Corporation met them fairly well indeed, but stipulated, a minimum of twenty loads per week at a certain price per load. ,On consulting the contractor to tilt Parish Council with regard to that stipulation it was ascertained that it was im- possible for him to carry it out. At the pre- sent time they were making inquiries of tlic Corporation if they would take ten loads per week. If that were accepted the contractor' could dispose in some other way the remaining ten loads. The contractor was short of men and could not do any more than he was doing at the present time. But if they satisfactorily over- came the difficulty of the disposal of the refuse from the pails they were still faced with the problem Of the *disposal of the ashes from, the dustbins. The Warrington Corporation could not take -their -ashes;- and as there Avere some- thing like 1,200 tini-to becmptied the question assumed a very 'Serious aspect. It meant that they Would have to get out of their difficulties -by; asking to 1)0 incorporated with the Borough of Warrington. They would be forced to do something on behalf of the ratepayers. The confines of ,their parish were very small. The provision of a destructor in an area of the kind would be a difficult matter, seeing that the district was so largely built upon. If they travelled outside their own parish to erect such -a destructor they would find landlords very reluctant to let land for such a purpose, as such erections were not a verT nice scene on the face of the country. In the next few years they would experience great difficulties, and the Parish Council would have to grapple with the problem and put it on a proper. basis. At pre- sent he could not see any solution. Every Home Its Own Destructor.

'Mr. J. Brown thought that a great deal of the trouble could be remedied by the residents burning large quantities of their rubbish. The contractor might report to the Council those people who refused to carry out the Council's instructions.

Mr. Pearson: I have inquired from the con- tractor if there was ally diminution of the rub- bish in the bins, and he had replied that there was very little.

In reply to a question as to the cost of re- moval by the Corporation, Mr. Pearson said the charge would be nd., per load, but they would not take ashes. However well tho Cor-

Back. to the Miles.

Mr. Bottomley : After taking all that ; into consideration we save still the ashes to con- sider.

Mr. Pearson: In aa week or two there will be nowhere to put the ashes, however clean they are from other rubbish.

Mr. Bottomley: There will he at least ten loals of ashes to dispose of each week.

Mr. Pearson :• I am afraid we shall have to call a parish meeting again before very long to go. into the whole question. On the proposition of Mr. Rose, seconded by Mr. Brown, the followinT:rosolution was passed: "That it be an instruction to all inhabitants in the parish that anything consumable by fir?, and garden refuse shall be refused by the con- tractor, and that the Connell: take steps to obtain a tip inside or outside the parish for such refuse as cannot be consumed:"

• An Irishman, who registered in August last. as Timothy Rabbit, aged 40 Years„ now claimed that he was 42, was charged at the Police Court. yesterday, before the Mayor, with being an absentee under the Military Service Act since March 23. When he was arrested it was dis- covered that he was a badged man,employed in the name of Timothy Fitzpatrick. . The Chief Constable pointed out that if any mistake had been made with regard to the age it was entirely the fault of the man himself. The police were • finding that the ages of a number of men had suddenly increased within the last nine months. The next they would be hearing about this man would be that he was back again in Galway. The case' was adjourned until McmThy for inquiries.

THE TWO FLANNERYS.

Thomas Flannery, of no settled' abode, pro- tested to the magistrates at the Police Court on Thursday that his previous convictions as read out by the Chief Constable were. not correct. He was charged with using obscene language. The Chief 'Constable stated that there Were 18 previous Convictions against the prisoner, in- cluding two for assaulting the police. . Prisoner: That's a lie; I have never been here for assaulting the police, as bad as I am. It is another man named Tom Flannery, who is dead and buried. The Chief Constable: Prisoner was here a month ago. Prisoner: I admit that if it is recorded against me. I only came out on March 13th. Flannery was sent to gaol for a month.

NOT GRANTED.

Elsie Hickman, 13, Howley-lane, applied for a separation order against her husband, John Hickman, on the ground of persistent cruelty. Complainant stated that on Saturday her hus- band came home drunk in the afternoon, threw a cup of scalding tea at her face, 'afterwards, turning her out of the house. This was - the only occasion he had been cruel to her. Defendant alleged that since his wife came back to live with him she had been worse .than ever for drink.

The magistrates., declined to grant a separa- tion order, as, in their- opinion, the ease was one of assault only.

LANCASHIRE COMBINATION.

WARRINGTON TOWN V. ALTRINCHAM.

The return fixture • between the above clubs was played last week on the -Hood-lane ground. There would be about 1,500 spectators present when the teams turned out as advertised:—

Warrington Town: Kite; Roberts and Leah; Broster, Whitfield. and Bullen ; Aspinall, Jones, Foster, Haywood and Cookson. -- Altrincham: MaHinson; Dickens and John- stone; Siddall, Cavanagh and Brogden ; Aldred,: Drinkwater, Best, llilditch and Greenhalgh. Referee, Mr. Lightbown, Manchester. The home captain won the toss and decided' to .defend the .lane goal. Best set the ball in motion, and Fester intercepted a pass. Hay- wood 'broke away, and forced Johnstone. to con- cede a corner. This was cleared by the visitors' defence, but in the next minute the home for- wards were again in front of the visitors' goal, this time .Aspinall placed wide. From the goal- kick the visitors made a run into the home quarters, ably led by Aldred, and Drinkwater placed the ball over the dead line. The visi- tors were now having the hest: of the game, I and the home backs were often ifi'difficulties owing.. to the speedy invasions by Aldred and Greenhalgh. A brief visit to the visitors' end saw Dickens clear with a big lunge, and Aldred neatly missed to Best, for him-to test Kite with a low shot, which the spectators thought had crossed the line. But Kite made a dive' along the ground and pushed the ball past the post, the spectators loudly applauding the clearance. A little later Greenhalgh was too high with a bard drive. The goal-kick gave relief to the home defence, and after midfield pity the horns forwards attacked. Mallinson made several good clearances. A free-kick to the visitors enabled them to take up the offensive again, Greenhalgh leading the way, and first Roberts and then Leah cleared, and the attack was ended by _Aldred hesitating to make his final effort. A spell of,even play followed. in wicb the honours were equally divided. Then Green-

halgh again led a raid on the. home goal, and from his centre Drinkwater and Aldred mulled one of the easiest openings, with only kite to beat. • Leah got the ball away and Whitfield • sent out to Aspinall, and. the. latter was a shade too wide with a good attempt. Directly after the interval was called wihout any goals being scored. •

On resuming the wind was much stronger, and of more assistance to the visitors, and the home .defence were soon called upon to clear. The relief was only temporary,. and the visitors, returned to the attack, but their final e.ofts were not steady enough to be effective. Follow- ing a few exchanges in midfield, Cookson trans- ferred to the opposite end, and after a slight check by Dickens, Cookson shot .hard into t,e side net. Responding to the call of the spec- tators, the home forwards attacked very strongly, a corner-kick ressulting, which was cleared by the backs, and a race to the home end, Hilditch forcing Leah to concede a-corner. This was well, placed by Brogden, and Best headed into the net. After this. reverse the exchanges were very keen, the home team making desperate • efforts to-get on equal terms,. but Mallinkon, Dickens and Johnstone shone in defence and kept their citadel intact. 'Eventu- ally the home team were gradually driven back and Aldred raced.away and forced a corner-kick at the, home end. This was placed wide owing to the strong Wind which was now blowing, and a little later Brogden shot over the bar. From, the goal-kick the 'home .forwards made 'another strong. .effort - to lower the visitors' ca/ours.- but , their attempt was in vain, as there always appeared to be an opponent ,wait- ing to check their movements. Then a clear- ance by • Cavanagh enabled Drinkwater to secure the ball just inside his own half of the, field, and he raced away on his own and beat all opposition and registered the visitors' second. goal. Just after this a heavy storm broke over the ground and spectators quickly c left the ground, and the remainder of the play was- not very interesting, and at the call of time the scores were :—Altrincham, 2 goals; Warrington Town, 0. •

IN THE COURTS.

10TORIST CHOOSES LESSER OF

TWO EVILS.

John Slater, 14, Rate e- oad. Haulgh, n-ar Bolton, was summoned ior driving a motorcar on Sunday March 19th. withalt either from. or year lights ten minutes after lightlu up time. Mr. W. B. Forshaw, wh3 dele ded, said hs client would have been in Eo:to.i long b fore lighting up time but for -s3me trouble with his car between Warrington and Frodsham. There were bead lights oil the car, but know- ing that they were not allowed to be lighted, he chose the lesser of the two evils and drove without. When the officer s'opped him he im- mediately garaged the car and proceeded home by train. On giving a donation of £1 to tl,e poor box and paying the. costs, 129., the charge was withdrawn.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0195.txt | included=true | included =====
DEAF AND DUMB?

CORONER SAYS THERE ARE SUSPICIOUS CIRCUMSTANCES.

The woman was present at the inquest pro- ceedings on .Tuesday. The. Coroner informed the jury. that it • was his intention to adjourn the inquiry, for a fort- night, in order that a post-mortem examination of the body of the. child might. be made. In consequence of an anonymous letter received by Inspector Wainwright, Of the N.S.P.C.C., in Which it was alleged that a• child taken in, and nursed by the woman, was in .a neglected con- dition, a visit was paid by the inspector to her house on Wednesday, March 22nd. The child was found asleep in. bed •apparently ill. The woman informed the inspector it was her own child and that it was suffering :from an attack of bronchitis through Cutting teeth. On the fol- lowing day Inspector Wainwright, in company ' with Mr. J. Constable (the infant life protection officer) •enquired why the child had not been regigtered with the local authority. Jt was ascertained in the meantime that the child was not her own, but one that she had taken -to be nursed. As the child had not been medi- cally attended, as advised by the officer on his first Visit, it was taken to the workhouse hospital, where it was found to be suffering from pneumonia. It was in A poor condition, and much below the normal weight of a child of its age. The condition of the child gradually grew worse, and it died on Monday last. The police officer who reported on the. case said that Dr. Binns, the acting medical officer at the hospital, was not satisfied as to the circumstances preced- ing death.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0196.txt | included=true | included =====
Adopted Five Children in Four Years.

There were, continued the Coroner, very sus- picious circumstances in the case.. Within the past four years the woman had Adopted no fewer than five children, three of whom had died. ' None of the five children was registered, as re- quired by the Children's Act of 1908. In November of last year the police reported to him the death of a child named Doris Good- win, aged seven weeks, who was stated to be the daughter of Charles and Ellen Goodwin. He dispensed with an inquest, but he and the police seemed to have been deceived. . The report in that case was that this child, Doris, was a fairly healthy girl, and was brought Up on cow's milk, as the woman was unable to feed it herself, and had to work to maintain the family. The child caught a chill. On the night of November 25 the mother retired to bed with the child (at that time, added the Coroner, the police and himself thought that Mrs. Goodwin was the mother). About five o'clock the next Imorning Mrs. Goodwin got up with the inten- tion of going to Work, took up the child to feet! it. when it sstretched itself out in her arms and died. She at once went for Dr. Liston, who lived close by, and after seeing the Child he ex- pressed the opinion to the police that it died from convulsions, probably brought on by a

slivht attack of bronchitis. In that case the police stated 'there were Po suspicious circum- stances; and, therefore, there were no facts which would have justified holding an inque4 It now transnired that Doris was not the child of 'Mrs. Goodwin.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0197.txt | included=true | included =====
Received from Liverpool and London.

In the case of the other child, name 4 Frank Goodwin, aged three months, who was certified by Dr. 'Manson to have died from meningitis, it was registered at birth and death as that of her own child, and he (the. Coroner) understood from the 'Police. that that was also untrue. With regard to the present inquiry the Woman first declared that it was her own child, but later- she admitted that it was not. All these children (added the Coroner) had been received from a Liverpool nurse, and in each instance Mrs. Goodwin had received a sum of money, usually about SAO. The child referred to in the anonymous letter was received by Mrs. Goodwin on March 17, from a nursing home in London. It was born on February 28th,, and was. quite well at the present time. . Under the, circumstances the jury would agree with him that it was. absolutely necessary that there should be a post-mortem examination of the child who had died on Monday. It was also only fair to. the woman, if she so desired, that she should. be represented at that exami- nation.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0198.txt | included=true | included =====
THE QUESTION OF REGISTRATION.

Inspector Wainwright, of the N.S.P.C.C., in reply to the Coroner, said the woman admitted to him that the child was not her's, • but she could not give the. name. It came from a nurse in Liverpool. When he visited the house he found four children—Lily, aged 12 years; Ethel, four years; and Charles (the deceased), two years; and a -baby, aged three weeks. The woman (and her husband) admitted that .three of them were not her children, but claimed that the elder girl, Lily, was her own child. When instituting inquiries he examined, by permis- sion of the -medical officer of health, the returns of the .births and deaths. Ile discovered that the death of the child Frank Goodwin, aged 27

days, Was registered on the certificate of Dr. Manson, but there was no record of the child's birth. He also found the. registration •of the death from convulsions of Doris Goodwin, aged 'seven weeks. He. searched for the record of the birth of Charles (the deceased), but failed to find it. Later he went to the office of the Registrar --(Mr, A. Bottomley); where he found that the Woman had registered the birth of the child Frank Goodwin as her own, born on July 17th, 1913.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0199.txt | included=true | included =====
CORONER'S ADVICE.

-The . Coroner (addressing the woman): The inquest will be adjourned for a fortnight, and if you will take advice from me you Will be represented by someone. •On the face of it things, look had against you, but the jury and I have a. perfectly open mind in this case. , The inquiry was adjourned until Tuesday, April Alth, at. 10 15.

Altrincham Turn The Tables.

Last Saturday morning ,the conditions from a spectator's point 'of view Were ideal for game of football, but, alas: as noon drew nearer they were not k) bright, and at the time of the kick-off 'at the Hood-lane ground it was very dull and threatening for rain, and there was a very cold wind: There was a good attendance of 'spectators when the teams turned out as advertised.

St. John's player, and also a member of the Crewe Combination team. Foster took the centre-forward position.

The visiting team, also had to make several changes from the previous week, Whitehead lying unable to play owing to injuriei. Dickens proved a capable ,substitute. Greenhalgh and Brogden changed places, and Aldred.came,in at the extreme right.

Then the visitors warmed to their work and caused the home defenders some -trouble. Greenhaigh and Aldred were the chief offenders, leading several spirited attacks and also show- ing a fine •turn of speed.

After each team had bad a turn ot attacking the game continued, fairly even. Midfield ex- challges were very keen, while near the goal the backs on both sides proved vep effective with one exception. Kite received 'well-earned applause for a splendid save with a low shot from 'Best. This appeared to settle the fate of the home players, • and they seemed somewhat dis- heartened; this was made More unsatisfactory owing to -a heavy rainstorm which continued till the call of time, when the result was : Altrincham; 2 goals; Warrington, nil.

FAIRFIELD UNITED RESERVES v. MERSEY JUNIORS.

The above tennis met last week on the Orford- road ground. PENKETH.

Wesley Guild.—On Tuesday evening the Wes- ley Guild held their annual general meeting in the Wesleyan Schoolroom. After electing the various committees, to act for the coming year, it was decided to hold the meetings once a month during the summer. There will also be a bowling match for ladies, and rambles to Chester, Frodsham and Lymm. Particulars will be decided on later. It is hoped by this means to keep in touch with the young people between the winter sessions. Mr. F. Davies was re-elected hon. secretary. The Rev. H. T. Dixon was in the chair.

Wedding.—On Saturday a marriage was sol- emnised at St. 'Mary's Church, Great Sankey, bettieen Miss Mary Watson, the youngest daughter of Mr. James Watson (who was, for more than 40 years, headmaster of Penketh. Council School) and of Mrs. Watson, and Mr. Arthur Patten, youngest son of Mr. and Mrs Patten, of Great Sankey. The Rev. J. R. Jones officiated. The bride wore a Saxe blue tailor-made costume, with grey hat, trimmed with wreath to match costume. Owing to the fact. that Mr. and Mrs. Watson have already two sons and two sons-in-law in the trenches, and that Mr. Patten himself is now in training- with the R.F.A., it was de- cided to have a quiet wedding. Only the very intimate friends and relatives gathered at the home of .the bride for the wedding breakfast. During the afternoon the newly-married couple left for a short honeymoon at Blackpool. A number of presents were received, including cheques or other cash equivalents to the value of presents friends would have bought Ahem- selves under less warlike circumstances.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0221.txt | included=true | included =====
LIGHTING CHARGES.

Questioned By Council; Adhered To By Corporation.

The statutory meeting of .the Penketh Parish Council was held on Wednesday evening, Coun- cillor J. G. Withinshaw in the chair. There were also present: Councillors R. Routledge, J. Woodward, J. Ditchfield, W. -Davis, W. Woods, Miss Tinsley, and Mr. E. Foster (clerk).

===== 0002601_19160401_art0222.txt | included=true | included =====
Expensive Breakages, Labour and Casts.

The following letters were read from Mr. W. S. Haddock, general manager of the Warring- ton Gas Department, with reference to com- plaints made by the Parish Council regarding excessive 'charges for. lighting the village with a minimum amount of lighting provided : "In reply to your letter of the 22nd, our expenses: on the lamps at Penketh have been the same as. if We had had the whole of them lighted; probably the cost has been rather more, as so many of the unlighted lamps have been wilfully broken. The credit of £6 6s. 6cl. is for the amount of gas not used through our not lighting the other lamps. Owing to extra wages and the cost of material, we have had a considerable loss on the lighting." "We are in receipt of your letter of the 26th and cannot agree that a detailed estimate of how our charge of 425. 6d. is made up would assist you to arrive at a decision as to the amount of gas that has been saved. If you wish to know how many lamps were out, how many days and how much gas was thereby saved, we can give you that information, but the . allowance of £6 6s. 6d. is based on the saving of gas worked out at 2s. 7d. per thou- sand feet."

On the motion of Councillor W. Davies, sec- onded by Councillor W. Woods, the Clerk was instructed to press for an exact account of charges. _ _ _

===== 0002601_19160401_art0223.txt | included=true | included =====
Election of Officials.

A letter from Dr. T. A. Murray was read saying that he did not wish to continue in office as an Overseer.

The appointments made were :--Chairman, Councillor J. G. Withinshaw; vice-chairman, Councillor R. Routledge; Overseers, Councillors J. G. Withinshaw, J. Ditchfield and T. Davies; Education Sub-committee, Dr. T. A. Murray, and, failing his acceptance, Councillor W. Davies.

Rats, Moles, Sparrows and Wood Pigeons Negligible.

A letter was read from the Ctencil in regard. to the destruction of rats, moles, sparrows and wood pigeons by organised methods; but the members hardly felt that it applied to this district

It was decided that no Parish Meeting would be held this. year, as the circumstances did not justify its existence. The next 'statutory m4tings are to be held' in July, October, January and March.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0224.txt | included=true | included =====
Visit of the Rev. W. A. Beckett.

There were large congregations at Bewsey- road Wesleyan Church on Sunday, on the occasion of the viAit of the Rev. W. A. Beckett. their late pastor. Special music was rendered under the leadership of Mr. J. E. Hannan, with Mr. George Pendlebury at the organ. In the morning the choir sang "110 w lovely are the messengers" l(Mendelssohn), and in the even- ing, "Ho watching over Israel" (Mendel- ssohn), and "By Babylon's wave" (Gonnod). Madame Sara Silcock and Mr. Sephton were the vocalists, and Mr. E. A. Murphy, of Pen- keth, presided.

ATTACK ON WARRINGTON TRIBUNAL.

Harold G. Brandwood (26), 28, Percy-street, a furniture salesman, claimed exemption on religious .and humanitarian grounds. The Warrington Tribunal, he alleged, recognised no conscientious objector. The conscience clause there was an absolute dead letter. Their insincerity, nay, their callousness, in the ad- ministration of the conscience clause is only exet:.ect,:ti their des:potism. he ho ti believed a man professinf, Christ should re- ceive more justice, but he could not receive less if he was an atheist.

The Lord Mayor: Do you belong to any Peace Society?—l belong to the No-Conscrip- tion.Fellowship.

What is it?—lt is against Conscription. No question of conscience about that?—That has nothing to do with my individual con- science.

Why did not you go for medical examina- tion?—lt was a back way of getting out, and I did not choose to go. I have a conscientious objection.

Do you belong to any religious body?—l am secretary of the Warrington Ragged School, which is undenominational. Appellant was exempted from combatant service, leave to appeal being refused.

Being on railway maintenance work, he could Carry out his duty to the State without violating his eouscience. He believed war to be murder, and he could not outrage his convictions by taking • the life of any man. He still had religious convictions, though he did not attend any place of worship. _ _ . _

CONVICTIONS FROM CHILDHOOD.

Samuel Smith (20), of 25, Roome-street, an export clerk employed by the Erasmic Soap Company, appealed on conscientious grounds. He was accompanied by his father.

In his appear, the appellant said he was fully convinced it was not God's will he should have any part or lot in carnal warfare, 'therefore he, would not directly participate in the war, and. he was prepared ,to suffer the consequences. The Warrington tribunal reported that the appellant early on made inquiries with a view to entering the Naval Sick Berth Reserve. Appellant now told the Tribunal that as the result of his inquiries, he did not have any- thing to do with it. Ho did not inquire about Lot, .

What are your conscientious objections now? —I am a Christian, and cannot go to take part in the war.

I am a Christian.—But it is not God's work to participik“ any Iva:.

Is an essential principle of Christianity sac- rifice for others?—Yes, in a way. Are you prepared to make a tiacrifleo for your country?—ln which direction.

Are you prepared to fight for your country:- - No. •

You do not wish to try to save the country from ruin?—No, I go to a far better country than this.

How long have you had these conscientious objections?--From childhood, my father taught me from the Scriptures.

Do you go to Church or Chapel?—l did go to the Gospel Hall in Warrington, hut not now.

You do not go anywhere?—No. Appeal dismissed. Applicant:. May I appeal? The Lord Mayor: No.

THE ONLY ANSWER.

William E. Wright,. 30, Marsh House-lane, railway stores clerk, said he could not take the military oath, because he refused to give up his freedom of conscienel. lie- could not take non-combatant service with the military oath, because he would then become -involved in the military machine. Appellant put in several letters from min- isters and others to show that he had held conscientious objections to war for some years. He added that he had asked himself what as right for him, he had counted the cost, and the only answer he could give was that he could have nothing to do with this horrible• crime.

Mr. Muspratt (a member of the Tribunal): What service would you undertake?—l am prepared to undertake anything in my power to bring about immediate peape and save tho lives of my fellows.

The Lord Mayor: If the Germans attempted to invade England, would you do anything to stop them coming?—l don't know what I should do.

Appellant Was sent to non-combatant serviee AMBULANCE MAN'S OBJECTIONS.

Fred Broadhurst, 39, Marsh House-lane, said he definitely refused to take the military oath, because war was inconsistent with the teaching of Christ. Taking part in such work would violate his conviction. Conscience he described as an innate sense of what was right and wrong, which no man-made law could affect. He was a member of the St. John Ambulance Association at Fazackerley, and left because the Colonel wished him to become part and parcel of the military maehine by taking the oath, which he refused to do.

The Lord Mayor: What is there to prevent you going for non-combatant 6ervice?—l hnl prepared to assist my country on condition that I don't become part of the military machir". Appeal dismissed.

FOR COMBATANT SERVICE.

Mr. T. Hobson, 7, Manchester-road, Hey- wood, appeared to support the appeals of five Christadelphians--Ernest Laycock, 162, Well- field-street; William Ludgate, 2, Orchard-street; A. Whittingham, 43, Lovely-lane; William M. Dagnall, 35, Oxford-street, Latchford; and H. Greenhalgh, 25, Dixon-street.

Mr. Hobson was proceeding to make a. speech, trying to impress the Tribunal, he said,, that these were not ordinary mass.. - • _ _ _ _

The Lord Mayor: We want to hear each individual as to his conscience.

Tho Lord Mayor proceeded to question Lay- cock, who said he would do anything in a civil 'capacity, but not military,

• - w d that both Lay-cock and Dagnall were wiredrawers.

Dagnall was then questioned. He said that ns a Christadelphian he was forbidden 'o take up tho sword, for " whosever taketh up the sword will die with the sword." They were as a separate people, dissociated from the world, and they lived on a much higher plane

"than you gentlemen." They lived according' to the commands of Christ. The appellant ex- plained that. he was on munition work, because he served, five years previous to the war on wiredrawing, and he did not hold himself re- sponsible for what .men were doing with the wire he drew. He could not help what orders he got: If the Tribunal thought he was doing wrong he was quite- willing to give up his situation.

The Lord Mayor said the Tribunal had con- sidered the cases of Laycock and Dagnall very carefully, and had decided to vary the decision of the local Tribunal and make an order for combatant service.

Mr. Hobson: I suppose we can appeal against that decision?

The. Lord Mayor 'No. Ludgate's case was 'taken next, the Lord Mayor at the outset sating he thought he ought to go for non-combatant: service. The appellant said. it was plainly shown in the Scriptures- that Some day all nations would fight against Christ's return to rule the. world, and they knew He would 'return sometime after the dying away of the Turkish Empire, which was taking place at present. When Christ took His place in the world, they knew the nations, including England, would oppose it. It would be a military force that would do it,, and they eould not be a party to that. They had sworn allegiance to Christ and must stand by the consequences. • • The Lord Mayor: We. dismiss. .the appeal. You mu-A take non-combatant price.

A. Whittingliam did not appear, and Mr. Hobson spoke on his. behalf. Appeal dismissed. In Greenhalgh's cage it appeared .that he was a clerk. He said he could not accept the decision of the local Tribunal to put him inter non-combatant service, for by to doing it would make him part of the military machine. The Lord Mayor: We shall vary the decision in this case, and make an order that you go for combatant service.

COMFORT FOR A FATHER.

A Corporation employe supported the appli- cation of his eon,, who is a tram conductor. He said three of, his sons joined at the outbreak of the war and two had been killed in action. He was a widower, and the son for whom he appealed ,was the • only one left at home. He would laci 19 years of age on Thursday (the next day). In announcing their decision, the Chairman remarked that it must be a great pride that two of the man's sons had given their lives for their country, although it was also a source of great sorrow. As the next day was the lad's birthday,, it would be some comfort to the father to know that the lad was granted con- ditional' exemption.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0232.txt | included=true | included =====
MARINE STORE DEALER AND HIS BROTHER.

In the case of an Allen-street marine store dealer, who appealed, it was stated that a re- quest had been sent to another brother, who had recently gone to Ireland, to forward a .doctor's certificate as to why he remained tross the Channel, but owing to the mails being over- due •through the storm, there had been no answer. _ '

The case was adjourned a fortnight, the ap- plicant being told to produce a doctor's certi- ficate on that occasion. _ _

Mr. T. S. Steel, solicitor: who represented the applicant, said that as soon as he knew ,a certificate was required for that sitting of the Tribunal, he took steps to have one obtained. That was on Monday. The Clerk: That was cutting it. a bit too fine in time. The Chairman In a fortnight we said like to see this other brother, but, in .any case, produce a medical certificate.

CLERKS EXEMPTED CONDITIONALLY. Four clerks—one in the order department and the others in the accountancy department —employed at Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons, Ltd., were granted three months' exemp- tion, the hope being expressed by the Chairman that an attempt would be made to make arrangements to release them:. Of the 147 clerks eligible for military service at the be- ginning of the war, 107 had either joined the Army or had attested. Twenty-five more had endeavoured to enlist, but had been rejected as Medically unfit. In the places of those who had gone they had installed 90 girls.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0233.txt | included=true | included =====
CORRESPONDENCE CLERK TO GO.

Application on behalf of four clerks at Messrs. Greening's was made. The cost and order clerk, and two who de the 'clerical work of the wire-weaving department, were condi- tionally exempted, while the correspondence clerk has to serve in the Army. The manager mentioned that owing to shortage of labour fourteen out ,of twenty-nine 'machines were standing. OUTFITTER TO BE MEDICALLY EXAMINED.

A resident of Stockton Heath, who carries on business as a ladies' outfitter and draper, in. Bridge-street, Warrington, applied for exemp- tion. He was represented by Mr. C. H. Uns- worth, solicitor, who said the applicant had put his life's oaVings. into the business. He was a fairly large ratepayer, and the difficulty was that he had not got any other male em- ploye. If he had to go into the Army it meant that the business/would be sacrificed entirely. He had been told that there had been several similar exemptions in Warrington. The Chairman: We will deal with this one by itself, on its merits. Mr. Unsworth : I am told it is so, but I won't press that. _ .

The Clerk: I have been told that we sent this case four months ago, according to what Warrington says, so you must not take, too much notice of it. Mr. Unsworth added that his client was physically unfit, and from what the doctor who examined him stated, he was under the impres- sion that he would be recommended for non- combatant service only, as he suffered from weakness of the insteps and ankles. He thought that if the Army wanted men for non-com- batant work, there were plenty of sources yet untapped, without taking a man who was a large ratepayer and who had put his all into his business. The rental of the shop was £lO year, and it was assessed at -260. The Chairman: We will adjourn this for a medical examination to be made. If a man is of no use as representing one bayonet he will probably be of more use in his business at home.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0234.txt | included=true | included =====
SINGLE OR MARRIED BROTHERS TO CHOOSE.

Frederick John Marsden, pork butcher, of Market-street, applied on behalf of himself and an employe. He stated that since the begin- ning of the war seven employes had left, four having joined the Army and three having gained employment in munitions works. It was pointed out to Mr. Marsden, who is married, that his brother had already been provisionally exempted, . and the Chairman added: "The Tribunal is of opinion that one of you ought to go. Your brother is single apd you are married. We Will adjourn it for a week for you to make up your minds as to which should go. As you are married and he is single, we think your brother will probably see his duty and let you off." The employe was exempted until July 31st, on grounds of domestic hardship.

WOMEN MANAGERS NOT ALWAYS

SUCCESSFUL

Conditional exemption was granted the man- ager of the Bridge-street shop of Messrs. Free- man, Hardy and Willis. He is a married man and has four brothers serving, one having been medically rejected. A member of the ilrm mentioned that over 600 of their employes had enlisted from their various branches. They had women in ,a great many of their shops, but they were not*.a suc- cess in the managing of big shops. They were all right for the smaller ones, but not for those which were important, such as that in Warrington. MASTER BUTCHER'S DILEMMA.

A master butcher and slaughterman told the Tribunal that he purchased his business after being refused admission into the Army as a butcher in July last. Otherwise, he would not have done 60. If he entered the 'Army in the ordinary way, his wife could not manage on 17s. 6d. a week, with rent of 10s. to pay. If the shop had to be closed up their savings were gone. An exemption until May 31st was granted, Captain Mathams remarking to the applicant that between now and that time it was quite possible be would be able to get into the butchery department in the Army.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0236.txt | included=true | included =====
APPEALS TO COUNTY TRIBUNAL.

More Promotions To Combatant Service.

Forty-three appeals from decisions given by the Warrington Tribunal came before the County Tribunal, at the Town Hall, Liverpool, on Thursday. The' Lord Mayor presided,. and amongst the members present was the Mayor of Warrington (Alderman Peter Peacock). In only three of the cases were flip decisions varied, one being the msult of an appeal by Captain Mathams, the military representative, against a month's exemption having been given. This exemption was now refused. In another case, the Tribunal gave a month's exemption, and in the third three Months. There were 18 appeals on conscientious grounds. All were rejected, and in three cases combatant service was substituted for , non-combatant. The conscientious grounds were, in the main, very similar to these put before the War- rington Tribunal.

===== 0002601_19160401_art0237.txt | included=true | included =====
DEMANDED A FORM.

Owen Owen (`.), a box-maker, of 34, Pinners- brow, stated that he had a •deep conscientious conviction that war was incompatible with the doctrine laid down by Jesus Christ, which doc- trine had been taught him from childhood. He would not attempt to kill- human life, and could not accept anything ,but absolute exemp tion from military serfice. The Warrington Tribunal reported that they were not prepared to accept the evidence as to Owen's conscientious objection, which seemed of recent growth. The Lord Mayor: Is your father a conscien- tious objector?—l don't know about him. It does not matter about him. It is what I am You have no idea?—No. Appellant said he worked at a controlloit establishment.

It is not against your conscience to work for theinP—No. not exactly. What Ido now I did taior to the ware

Did you offer to resign your work when the works became controlled?--Ye& You offered to resign—No; I did not offer to then.

Do you think it is right to preserve and save life ?—Yes, it is right to save life. If anyone was attacked, would you defend him?—l would try to disarm them, but I woull not take life even to save life. Appeal dismissed. Appellant demanded an appeal form. , The Lord Mayor: You cannot demand one. If you ask in a proper manner the Clerk • will give you one, but we decline to give you leave to appeal.

A STRIKE LEADER AND "BLACKLEGS."

Thomas Howard (26), 3, Frederick-street, a window cleaner, said it would be a great viola- tion of his conscience if he participated in any movement which he believed VS be working against the highest and noblest aims of human- ity: The world was his country, mankind his brethren, to do good his religion. The Warrington Tribunal stated that the appellant was a member of the No-Conscription Fellowship, a Socialist, a Materialist, and said he owed no duty to the country. In view of these statements made by the appellant wliNi before the Warrington Tribunal, the Tribunal doubted if he had a conscience at all.

The Lord Mayor said they had nothing to do with what the appellant's politics were. Ile had to satisfy the Tribunal, if he could, that he had a conscientious objection. The appellant said that since he ...waS•l9...he., had followed the Independent Labour partY, whose politics had mainly been anti-military. On several occasions he had attended meetings that had been opposed to tho military policy of the Government. lie believed that all war, as expressed by national antagonism, was wrong. By trade he was a light metal wqrlor and had worked at Messrs. Fletcher, Russell's, and other firms.

The Lord Mayor: When did you leave Fletcher, Russell's?—August, 1914. Why did you leave?—l was not satisfied with the job. I did not feel physically fit. Captain Dickinson (the military representa- tive): Before you left Fletcher, Russell's, were you leader of a strike?—l was on a Strike Com- mittee.

You were leader of what is known as the Physical Force party?—No. The Lord Mayor: Were you leader of the strike?—l was on the Strike Committee, elected by the men going on strike. Were there any blacklegs there?—There were one or two working. In the usual strike voca- bulary, they were blacklegs. • Any men forcibly stopped working?—No, peaceful picketing. You would go for a blackleg, would you not? —No, he might be a better fighter than myself. (Laughter.) Would that be the ground of your conscien- tious objection?—No, the ground of my reason- ing. I agree that would be a better reason than a conscientious objection, but if a blackleg was a small man you would go for him?—No, I would put the points of our case to him, to his right honest common-sense. Captain Dickinson: At the time of the strike did not you go about with a heavy stick or bludgeon?—l had a stick: • • A heavy one?—No, a very light one. The Lord Mayor: Did not you come with a stick to-day?—No, to tell you the truth I have not got one now. I cannot afford ono. The Lord Mayor: .We are not'. satisfifd -You have a conscientious objection, and we dismiss the appeal. Appellant: On the principle involved.,• I ask to be allowed to appeal further. The Lord Mayor: There is no question of principle involved. It is a question of fact. Appellant: Can I have a form?—Yes. hut you have applied for leave and we have de- clined.

REFUSED TO TOUCH SHELLS.

Thomas Taylor (20), 2, Priestley-street, a fitter, put forward as proof that he V78...4 sincere in his conscientious objection that he was in a reserved occupation. . lie added that in the shop where he worked they were making shells, and he had refused absolutely to touch them. If a man refusqd to handle shells, be would refuse to take life. Re could have got off absolutely had he attested. Harold Wilson, 32; N. orri's-street, a fitter on the Cheshire Lines, produced letters from min- isters and others as to his conscientious objec- tions to war. He said he believed war to be wrong. He was assisting his country by doing railway work.—Appeal dismissed. FR/ENDS 114 GERMANY.

John Richardson, 48, Mill-lane; of no occupa- tion, repeated the objections he stated at War- rington. Under no Circumstances could he take part in the •taking of human life. He had been in Germany many time,;, and had always been very kindly treated, and when the .war was over he should certainl3- go again. He adniired the German people from what he had seen, but did not admire the 'part they were playing in the war.----APpeal dismieFsed. GENERAL APPEALS.

Mr. T. S. Steel appeared in support of two appeals. In one a. traincar conductor WRS given a month's exemption, during which time it was said his mother would probably- undergo an operation. In the other, the son of a. furniture dealer, coal dealer and general carting con- tractor was exempted for three months to en- able the father to make other arrangements for the conduct of his business.

Mr. A. Browne supported the appeal of an- other coal dealer and carting contractor for the exemption of his stepson. 't'he local Tri- bunal, however, reported that business could be carried on without: the son, al4l the appeal was dismissed.

The conductor cf the Theatre Orchestra was exetnpted, for three months. His only brother had been killed ,in action, and he was now the only support of his Widowed mother. Captain Mathums appealed against the deci- sion Of the Warrington Tribunal in allowing one month's exemption to the son of the Parish Church clerk, Who, it was claimed, was depend- ent upon him for assistance in the Carrying out of his duties. The Rector wrote asking for the careful consideration of the case. The Lord, Mayor said the Tribunal had listened very carefully to the appellant's case, but they felt they, must allow. the appeal. That meant the appellant got no exemption at all.

DISTRICT NEWS.

PAD GATE

Wesley Guild.—The closing social of the Wes- ley Guild was held on Monday evening, pre- sided over by the Rev. A. J. Morton.' 'Unfor- tunately the attendance was not go large as usual, but, nevertheless, an enjoyable evening had been arranged by the Social Committee. 'Mrs. J. Bennett sang "Killarney" and "Last Night"; and Miss Collins, of Bold-street, re- cited "Handing Down" and "Paddy's Court- ing," also giving "But they didn't," as an encore. A competition, arranged by Mr. PEn- ston, proved an attractive part of the pro- gramme, the prize being won by Miss J. Stewart. Refreshments were provided.

Pupils' Concert. —A very interesting concert was 'given in the Wesley Hall •on Saturday afternoon by Miss Roscollor's pupils., The concert was presided over by Mrs. (Dr.) Bennett, who said that Miss Roseollor had offered to give the proceeds, to the Local Work- ing committee of the Relief Fund, and she, on behalf of the Committee, wished to thank her for her generosity. Whilst we were all willing to do what we could. to send comforts to our Soldiers at the Front, it was only nat- ural that we should look after the Patigate lads first. The concert on the whole. was very successful, and Miss Roscollor must be con- gratulated on her training of the young ehil- dren. Some, of the dress effects were 'very pretty and it was. evident that great Care had been taken- to make this part of the performanee attractive. An item which calls- for special mention was an Irish ballad, "Impudent Bar- ney O'Hea," which received a well-deserved encore. 'Miss Hilda Barker's singing was very enjoyable in " liullo, there's little • Tommy Atkins" and "Sergeant Daddy, V.e." Other items were :—Action songs, "Mister Shadow Man " and "The Golden Boat," by the pupils; (Net, "Look out, another," by Monica Rimmer and Elsie Powner; pianoforte solo, by Robert Emmett; and three country dances—lrish jig, Welsh dance, and Highland fling. An operetta entitled." Knavish Tricks" was also given, the principal characters being "King of Hearts," Elsie Powner; " Queen of Hearts," Dora Wil- son; "Knave of Hearts," Erie Walker; minor parts being taken by Jessie Taylor, Martha Hackett, Amy Redhead, Phyllis Carpenter, Sonny Bennett, Roland Smith, and chorus. Miss Elsie Powner and Master Eric Walker were exceptionally good throughout The audi- ence was exceptionally large, the hall being filled. Refreshments were served during the interval at a small charge. ,

Local Relief -Funes.—A wry intete,:ting ,lecture on" A Holiday Tour in Norway" - was given in the Wesley Hall on Thursday evening by De. Bennett. The meeting was pre- sided over by ,Mr. E. Gorton, of Woolston, Ay:10 said the. cause • they had 4 .he.art was well , worthy of Support., for the probet4.ls were'fo be used in sending out gifts to our local lads who. had gone to fight the nation's battles. The knowledge that these gifts had come from "home" increased their value. I Dr. Bennett paid a tribute to the substantial help ,and sympathy which the Chairman had given to the a'bunded soldiers in Ow Derby Hospital, Winwiek. Speaking on his leetnre, he said that the holiday in Norway was one , of the most delightful, he had over spent. The I scenery is unequalled and unsurpassed. The ' fiords are occupied by a race of people known as the Vikings. He gave a very intesting sketch of their history:. The native costumes were also a feature in many of the slides shown and the izmumerable waterfalls for which the country is noted were a source of pleasure. The atmosphere is so .clear and rarified that it is impossible to judge the height of the moun- tains. There are very few railways and in a distance of 70 miles there are t 3 tunnels, which show the difficultiee which the engineers have

had to encounter. he .principal industries are timber and fpfhing. The people are very industrious and very trustworthy, though. mostly of the peasant. dass. -Altogether, about 60 slides were shown, • which were of special interest, as many local residents wore included on the trictures. The Doctor incidentally men- tioned that during. his Stay in Mold° he Was privileged to. take .a snapshot of, the Kaiser,, who raisecl. his hat and went away smiling' after he had had his photo taken.

Writing froth the headquarters of, a Lanca, 1i ire Territorial Regiment, the reverend gentleman says, inter alia: "I am very Well, but very busy. The work is rather trying and nothing • ;nit here can wait-everything has to be done. when orderd. I have charge of two battalions. One of them is in ,the trenches at present and, is a fighting unit, whilst the other is at. .present employed in digging trenches, hoarding them, wiring, and other pretty dan- gerous work. Lae Friday I went to the fire frenetic- and was under fire for four hours. It wa, cot over piew,ant, but it was very in- teresting. I was shelled in the communication trenches, and also on the road ti-3 the village, which is treated to doses of shrapnel every day. The worst thing, however, is the burials. Last night I had to go to the back of the line to bury two of my poor boys, while bullets were spitting all around. Five Btrack- -the soil which lay 'heaped up on each side •of the grave. GOSSIP. The Town Council, while agreeing with the resolution of the Infirmary Working Men's Com- mittee, that a motor ambulance for Warrington is necessary, regret that at present the purchase cannot be made, as the cost has not been allowed for in the current estimates. The Council, however, hare ample time to make amends in this respect for next year. It has been rumoured this week that the local Territorials had been in another big fight, and suffered-seAtely. There is not an atom of truth in the rumour. . • Congratulations to Mr. J. IL Smethurst, who on Thursday was chosen Chairman Of the Board of Management of the Infirmary for the fifth time, thus setting up, I believe, a distinct record. • Mr. Steel was the subject of an extraordinary compliment from the Lord Mayor of Liverpool on Thursday. At the Appeal Tribunal Mr. Steel had been successful on a day when few appeals had been of any avail to his Lordship, and at the•close made pointed reference to the fact. Mr. Steel bowed his acknowledgments and retired. Unless special permission is given by the com- petent military authority, the church bells of Warrington and district will have to pass their nights in silence. The Home Office has issued a notice to the effect that the ringing and chiming of bells and the striking of clocks audible in any street or other open space is prohibited. In the quiet of the night it is feared that the striking of bells might acquaint Zeppelins of the whereabouts of a sleepy town or hamlet. In the immediate diatrict. the Order, there- fore, affects the Town Clock, and those at the ' Parish Church, the Blue Coat School, Walton Hall, and Stockton Heath Church. The night must be one of sitance. Soon we shall have a regulation forbidding snoring. -Mr. J. A. Sedd2n was announced to speak on the war at the Parr Hall, on Thursday. He also spoke on an empty stomach. He left London at 12.10, thinking of dining on the way down, but he was unable to obtain anything, and arrived at Warrington famished after a journey of nine hours owing to the dis- location of traffic through the blizzard. - He came straight to the Parr Hall, got to his "muttons" right away—in the sense that he did not waste words—and left his dining Until afterwards. As stated elsewhere, Mr. Seddon was an hour and a half late at the meeting. The way one speaker after another rose to fill in the time reminded one of an overflow meeting in elec- ion days. VIGILANT.
